On the Mixtures and Powers of Simple Drugs

De simplicium medicamentorum temperamentis ac facultatibus · Kühn XI 379-892 and XII 1-377 · books 1-11

No complete published translation into English by a scholar that we know of. An English translation of books 1-5 by J. Wilkins is announced in the Cambridge Galen Translations. Books 6-8: a French translation with the Greek by C. Manco (doctoral thesis, Montpellier and Bologna, 2020). The prefaces of books 6-11 and the first two entries of book 6: English by M. Coughlin (ancientmedicine.org, 2023). All eleven books: a free French version by H. Fayard (1548). An unreviewed machine translation into English of Kühn XI and XII, the volumes containing these books, was posted at Source Library (sourcelibrary.org) during 2026. Passages with a red rule failed the machine checks; those checks are automated agreement tests, not a reading of the Greek, so a passage without the rule is not checked either. Check (b): the two renderings agree on at least 70% of the unit's target terms. Check (c): a model judge, salted with 10 planted errors and 10 anchors it had to get right, finds at least 80% of the term renderings within the LSJ entry's senses, with at most 2 outside. A unit fails when it fails two of three.

Flags, under each passage: places for a reader to look, not confirmed errors. Sentence by sentence, each rendering is compared with the Greek for numbers, negations and words of more or less, and for the names of drugs and parts of the body (a name in the English with none in the Greek, or a name in the Greek with none in the English). These are word-matching tests: a flag often comes from a sentence pair that is aligned a little off, a paraphrase or a name the model kept in Greek, and a passage with no flag is not checked by them either. The flags play no part in the unit verdicts.

Simp.11.1.1Simp. 11.1.1, ch. 11.1 difficulty low (2.46/100; thin 27, absent 19) (a) fail (b) pass 7/8 (c) pass 14/14 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
11.1.1 [α΄. Περὶ σαρκὸς ἐχίδνης.] Οὐχ ἅπασαι τῶν ζώων αἱ σάρκες ἄνθρωπον τρέφουσιν, ἀλλ’ ἐνίων εἰσὶ καὶ θανάσιμοι τῶν φαρμακωδῶν οὐδὲν ἧττον, ἃ καλοῦσι δηλητήρια, καὶ τῶν τρεφουσῶν δὲ ἡμᾶς σαρκῶν ἔνιαι μὲν αὐτὸ τοῦτο μόνον εἰσὶ τροφαὶ, τινὲς δὲ πρὸς τῷ τρέφειν ἔχουσι καὶ τὴν ὡς φαρμάκου δύναμιν, ἐπειδὴ κατὰ τὸ ξηραίνειν ἢ ὑγραίνειν καὶ θερμαίνειν ἢ ψύχειν, ἀλλοιοῦσι τὸ σῶμα. τὰς γοῦν τῶν ἐχιδνῶν θερμαινούσας τε καὶ ξηραινούσας ἐναργῶς ἔστιν ἰδεῖν ἀρτυομένας, ὥσπερ αἱ ἐγχέλεις ἐλαίῳ καὶ ἁλσὶ καὶ ἀνήθῳ καὶ πράσῳ καὶ ὕδατι δηλονότι μετὰ τοῦ συμμέτρου. ὅτι δὲ καθαίρουσιν διὰ τοῦ δέρματος ἅπαν τὸ σῶμα γνῶναί σοι πάρεστι κᾀξ ὧν ἐπειράθην ἐγὼ ἔτι νέος γενόμενος ἐπὶ τῆς ἡμετέρας Ἀσίας, ὧν ἕκαστον ἐφεξῆς ἤδη δίειμι. ἄνθρωπος νοσῶν τὸ καλούμενον πάθος ἐλέφαντα μέχρι μέν τινος ὁμοδίαιτος ἦν τοῖς συνήθεσιν, ἐπεὶ δ’ ἐκ τῆς πρὸς αὐτὸν κοινωνίας τε καὶ ὁμιλίας ἐκοινώνησαν μὲν ἔνιοι τοῦ πάθους, αὐτὸς δὲ δυσώδης ἦν ἤδη καὶ εἰδεχθὴς, καλύβην αὐτῷ πηξάμενοι πλησίον τῆς κώμης ἐπὶ χθαμαλοῦ τοῦ λόφου παρά τινι πηγῇ ἱδρύουσιν ἐν αὐτῇ τὸν ἄνθρωπον φέροντες τροφὰς αὐτῷ ἐφ’ ἡμέρας τοσαύτας ὅσον ἀποζῆν ἱκανῶς. περὶ δὲ κυνὸς ἐπιτολὴν θερισταῖς πλησίον αὐτοῦ θερίζουσιν ἐκομίσθη τις οἶνος ἐν κεραμίῳ μάλ’ εὐώδης. ὁ μὲν κομίσας ἐγγὺς τῶν θεριζόντων καταθεὶς ἐχωρίσθη· τοῖς δ’ ὡς ἧκεν ὁ καιρὸς τοῦ πίνειν, ἔθος μὲν ἦν αὐτοῖς ἐκχέουσι κρατῆρα μεθ’ ὕδατος συμμέτρου κεραννύναι τὸν οἶνον, ὡς δὲ ἀνελομένου νεανίσκου τὸ κεράμιον, ἐξαιροῦντά τε τὸν οἶνον εἰς τὸν κρατῆρα, συνεξέπεσεν ἔχιδνα νεκρά. δείσαντες οὖν οἱ θερισταὶ μή τι πάθοιεν ἐκ τοῦ πόματος, αὐτοὶ μὲν ὕδατος ἔπιον, ὡς δ’ ἀπηλλάττοντο, χαρίζονται δῆθεν ὑπὸ φιλανθρωπίας τῷ τὸν ἐλέφαντα νοσοῦντι τὸν ὅλον οἶνον, ἄμεινον αὐτῷ κρίναντες εἶναι τεθνάναι μᾶλλον ἢ ζῇν τοιούτῳ. ὁ δ’ ἐκ τούτου πίνων ὑγιὴς ἐγένετο θαυμαστόν τινα τρόπον. ὅλον γὰρ αὐτοῦ τὸ τοῦ δέρματος ὀχθῶδες ἀπέπεσεν ὡς τῶν μαλακοστράκων ζώων τὸ σκέπασμα. ὅσον δ’ ὑπόλοιπον ἦν ἔτι μαλακὸν ἱκανῶς ἐφαίνετο καθάπερ τὸ τῶν καράβων τε καὶ καρκίνων, ὅταν ἀποπέσῃ τὸ πέριξ ὄστρακον. ἕτερον τοιοῦτον ἐξ ὁμοίας περιπτώσεως ἐγένετο κατὰ τὴν ἐν Ἀσίᾳ Μυσίαν, οὐ πόῤῥω τῆς ἡμετέρας πόλεως. ἄνθρωπος ἐλέφαντι κάμνων ἐπὶ χρῆσιν ὥρμησεν ὑδάτων θερμῶν αὐτοφυῶν ὠφελείας ἐλπίδι. παλλακὶς δ’ ἦν αὐτῷ δούλη νέα τε καὶ καλὴ πολλοὺς ἐραστὰς ἔχουσα, ταύτῃ καὶ ἄλλα μέν τινα τῶν κατὰ τὴν οἰκίαν, ἀτὰρ οὖν καὶ τὰ κατὰ τὸ ταμεῖον ἐπίστευεν ὁ κάμνων. ὡς δὲ καταχθέντων αὐτῶν, ἡνίκ’ ἐχρῆτο τοῖς ὕδασιν, ἐν οἰκίᾳ παρακείμενον ἐχούσῃ χωρίον αὐχμηρὸν ἐχιδνῶν μεστὸν, ἐμπεσοῦσά τις αὐτῶν εἰς οἴνου κεράμιον ἀμελῶς κείμενον ἐναπέθανεν, ἕρμαιον ἡγησαμένη τὸ κατὰ τύχην ἐκβὰν ἡ παλλακὴ τῷ δεσπότῃ τὸν ποτὸν ἐκ τούτου προσέφερεν. ὁ δὲ πίνων αὐτὸ τῷ κατὰ τὴν καλύβην ὡσαύτως ὑγιάσθη. δύο μὲν ἤδη σοι ταῦτα διδάγματα τῆς κατὰ τὴν περίπτωσιν πείρας, ἕτερον δ’ ἐπ’ αὐτοῖς τρίτον ἐξ ἡμετέρας μιμήσεως. ἐπειδὴ γάρ τις νοσῶν τοῦτο τὸ νόσημα φιλοσοφώτερος ἢ κατὰ τοὺς πολλοὺς ἐδυσχέραινέ τε δεινῶς καὶ τεθνάναι βέλτιον ἢ ζῇν ἔφασκεν εἶναι, διακειμένῳ οὕτως ἀθλίως ἐδήλωσα τὰς προειρημένας δύο περιπτώσεις αὐτῶν. ὁ δ’ , αὐτός τε γὰρ ἦν ἔμπειρος οἰωνῶν ἐχρήσατό τε φίλῳ θαυμαστῶς κατορθοῦντι τὸ μάθημα, καθίσας ἐπ’ ὄρνισιν ἅμ’ ἐκείνῳ προὐτράπη ﻿ τε μιμήσασθαι τὰ διὰ τῆς πείρας ἐγνωσμένα καὶ πίνων οἶνον οὕτω φαρμαχθέντα λεπρώδης ἐγένετο. χρόνῳ δ’ ὕστερον ἰασάμεθα καὶ τὴν λέπραν αὐτοῦ τοῖς συνήθεσι φαρμάκοις. τέταρτος ἐπὶ τούτῳ τέχνην πεποιημένος ἐχίδνας ζώσας συλλαμβάνειν ἐν ἀρχῇ μὲν ἦν τοῦ πάθους ἐκείνου, προὔκειτο δ’ ἡμῖν ὅπως ἰαθῇ τάχιστα. φλέβα τε οὖν αὐτοῦ τεμόντες καὶ καθήραντες φαρμάκῳ μέλαιναν κενοῦντι, χρήσασθαι ταῖς ἀγρευομέναις ἐχίδναις συνεβουλεύσαμεν, ἐν λοπάδι σκευάζοντι, καθάπερ τὰς ἐγχέλεις. οὗτος μὲν οὖν οὕτως ἐθεραπεύθη, διαπνεύσαντος αὐτῷ τοῦ πάθους. ἄλλος δέ τις ἀνὴρ πλούσιος οὐχ ἡμεδαπὸς οὗτός γε, ἀλλ’ ἐκ μέσης Θρᾴκης ἧκεν, ὀνείρατος προτρέψαντος αὐτὸν εἰς τὸ Πέργαμον, εἶτα τοῦ θεοῦ προστάξαντος ὄναρ αὐτῷ πίνειν τε τοῦ διὰ τῶν ἐχιδνῶν φαρμάκου καθ’ ἑκάστην ἡμέραν καὶ χρίειν ἔξωθεν τὸ σῶμα, μετέπεσεν τὸ πάθος οὐ μετὰ πολλὰς ἡμέρας εἰς λέπραν, ἐθεραπεύθη τε πάλιν οἷς ὁ θεὸς ἐκέλευεν φαρμάκοις καὶ τοῦτο τὸ νόσημα. ἡ μὲν δὴ τῶν ἐχιδνῶν σὰρξ εἰς τοσοῦτον ἥκει τῆς ξηραντικῆς δυνάμεως· ἐπεὶ δ’ ἔνιοι τῶν φαγόντων αὐτὴν ἑάλωσαν δίψει σφοδροτάτῳ καὶ διὰ τοῦτο προσαγορεύουσι τὰς ἐχίδνας διψάδας. εἰσὶ δ’ οἳ καὶ τοὺς δηχθέντας ὑπ’ αὐτῶν φασιν οὐκ ἐμπίπλασθαι πίνοντας, ἀλλὰ διαῤῥήγνυσθαι πρότερον ἢ παύσασθαι διψῶντας. διὰ τοῦτο τῶν ἐν Ῥώμῃ τὰς ἐχίδνας θηρευόντων, οὓς ὀνομάζουσι Μαρσοὺς, ἐπυθόμην εἴ τι σημεῖον ἔχοιέν με διδάξαι διακριτικὸν ἑκατέρου τοῦ γένους τῶν ἐχιδνῶν· οἱ δ’ οὐδὲν ὅλως ἔφασαν εἶναι γένος ἐχιδνῶν διψάδων, ἀλλὰ τὰς παρὰ θαλάττῃ καὶ τόποις ἁλμυρίδα πολλὴν ἔχουσι διαιτωμένας ἁλμυρὰν ἴσχειν τὴν σάρκα, διὸ καὶ κατὰ Λιβύην πολλὰς γίγνεσθαι τοιαύτας, ἐν Ἰταλίᾳ δ’ οὐκ εἶναι διὰ τὴν ὑγρότητα τῆς χώρας. ταῦτα μὲν οὖν ἤκουσα τῶν Μαρσῶν λεγόντων, οὐ μὴν ἔχω βεβαίως εἰπεῖν εἴτ’ ἀληθεύσουσι τὸ σύμπαν εἴτε καὶ ψεύδονται κατά τι. τὸ μὲν γὰρ ἐν οἷς εἰρήκασι χωρίοις γίνεσθαί τινας ἐχίδνας ἁλυκὴν ἐχούσας τὴν σάρκα πιθανώτατον εἶναί μοι δοκεῖ. συμμεταβαλλούσας γὰρ οἶδα ταῖς τροφαῖς τὰς τῶν ζώων σάρκας, οὐ μὴν ὡς οὐδέν ἐστι γένος ἐχιδνῶν διψάδων ἀποφήνασθαι δύναμαι. τὸ δ’ οὖν ἀσφαλέστατόν ἐστι φυλάττεσθαι τὰς ἐν τοῖς τοιούτοις χωρίοις ἐχίδνας θηρεύειν εἰς ἐδωδὴν ἢ φαρμάκου κατασκευὴν, ὁποῖόν ἐστι καὶ τουτὶ τὸ ἔνδοξον, ὃ καλοῦσιν ἅπαντες σχεδὸν ἰατροὶ θηριακήν. ἐπεὶ δ’ ἔθος ἡμῖν ἐστιν, ὅταν τοὺς καλουμένους ἀρτίσκους θηριακοὺς σκευάζωμεν, ἀφαιρεῖν οὐ μόνον τὴν κεφαλὴν αὐτῶν, ἀλλὰ καὶ τὴν οὐρὰν, ἐνενόησα πολλάκις εὐλόγως ἴσως μὲν τὴν κεφαλὴν ὅλην, διὰ τὸν ἐν τῷ στόματι περιεχόμενον ἰὸν, ἀλόγως δὲ τὴν οὐρὰν ἀφαιρεῖσθαι. οὐδὲ γὰρ τοῦτ’ ἔστιν εἰπεῖν, ὅτι διὰ τὰ περιττώματα τῆς τροφῆς τά θ’ ὑγρὰ καὶ τὰ ξηρὰ πρακτέον οὕτως ἐστίν. ἀποκτείναντες γὰρ αὐτὰς, εἶτ’ ἐκδείραντές τε καὶ ἀναπτύξαντες, ἐξαιροῦμέν τε καὶ ἀποῤῥίπτομεν ἅπαντα τὰ ἔνδον, ὡς μόνην καταλείπεσθαι τὴν τῶν σαρκῶν οὐσίαν ἅμα ταῖς διαπεφυκυίαις αὐτῶν ἀρτηρίαις τε καὶ φλεψὶν, ἐλάχιστον ἐχούσαις ὄγκον ὡς πρὸς τὴν ὅλην σάρκα, καὶ μηδὲ φαινόμενον, ἐὰν μὴ πάνυ τις ἐπιμελῶς κατασκέψηται. τοὺς μὲν οὖν ἀρτίσκους, οὓς δὴ καὶ θηριακοὺς ὀνομάζουσι, σκευάζομεν οὕτως. κεκαθαρμένας αὐτὰς λαβόντες, εἶθ’ ἕψοντες ἐν ὕδατι, μέχρις ἂν ἀκριβῶς ἡμῖν εἶναι δόξωσιν ἑφθαί. συνεμβάλλομεν δ’ εὐθέως ἐξ ἀρχῆς ἀνήθου τῷ ὕδατι καὶ μετὰ τὴν ἕψησιν ἀπὸ τῶν σαρκῶν διακρίνομεν τὰς ἀκάνθας, εἶτα μίγνυμεν ἄρτῳ λελειωμένῳ τὴν σάρκα. τὸν δ’ ἄρτον τοῦτον οὐ τὸν ἐπιτυχόντα λαμβάνομεν, ἀλλ’ ὡς ἔνι μάλιστα καθαρώτατόν τε καὶ καλῶς ὠπτημένον ἐν κλιβάνῳ, συμμέτρων ἁλῶν ἔχοντα καὶ ζύμης. προαναξηραίνομεν δὲ αὐτὸν ἐν οἴκῳ ξηρῷ καὶ ἀνίκμῳ, μέχρις ἂν οὕτω γένηται ξηρὸς ὡς ἐν ὅλμῳ κοπῆναι δύνασθαι. οὐ μὴν κόπτοντές γε μίγνυμεν, ἀλλὰ διαβρέχοντες τῷ ὕδατι, καθὸ τὰς ἐχίδνας ἑψήσαμεν. αὐτὴν δὲ τὴν σάρκα πρὶν μιγνύναι τῷ ἄρτῳ, τρίβομεν ἐν θυίᾳ τῶν μαγείρων, ἄχρις ἂν ἀκριβῶς γένηται λεία. καὶ μετὰ ταῦτα μικροὺς ἀρτίσκους λεπτοὺς πλάσαντες, εἶτα ξηράναντες ἐν οἴκῳ θερμῷ καὶ ξηρῷ φυλάττομεν ἀποτιθέμενοι πάλιν ἐν οἴκῳ τοιούτῳ. τούτους μὲν οὖν εἰσβάλλοντος τοῦ θέρους σκευάζομεν, ἡνίκα μάλιστα βελτίστη τῶν ἐχιδνῶν ἐστιν ἡ σάρξ. χρώμεθα δ’ ὕστερον κόπτοντές τε καὶ διάττοντες, εἶτ’ αὖθις λειοῦντες ἀναμιγνύντες τε τοῖς ἐσκευασμένοις εἰς ἡδονὴν ἁλσὶν, ἐμβάλλομεν δ’ αὐτῶν καὶ τῇ θηριακῇ. γίγνονται δὲ καὶ οἱ διὰ τῶν ὀπτηθεισῶν ἐχιδνῶν ἅλες ὑπὸ τὸν αὐτὸν καιρὸν εἶς χύτραν καινὴν ἐμβαλλόντων ἡμῶν τὰς ἐχίδνας ζώσας, ἅμα τοῖς ὑπεστορεσμένοις τε καὶ περικειμένοις αὐταῖς φαρμάκοις, ἃ λέγειν ἅπαντα νῦν οὐκ ἔστι τῆς ἐνεστώσης πραγματείας. ἴσως γάρ τις ἡμῖν εὐλόγως ἐγκαλέσει καὶ περὶ τῶν ἀρτίσκων τῆς κατασκευῆς ὡς οὐκ ἐν καιρῷ διελθοῦσιν. ἀλλὰ ταῦτα μὲν ἐπειδὴ φθάνει λελέχθαι, φυλαττέσθω, κᾂν δοκῇ μὴ πάνυ τι τῆς προκειμένης εἶναι πραγματείας ἴδια. τὰ δ’ ἑξῆς κατὰ τὸ προσῆκον μέτρον λεγέσθω. προσῆκον δ’ ἐστὶ περὶ τῆς καθόλου δυνάμεως ἀναμνῆσαι τὴν σάρκα τῶν ἐχιδνῶν, εἰπόντα εἶναι ξηραντικήν τε καὶ διαφορητικὴν ἰσχυρῶς, ἄμα τῷ θερμαίνειν μετρίως. ἐπείγεται δ’, ὡς ἔοικεν, ἡ δύναμις αὐτῆς ἐπὶ τὸ δέρμα, διὰ τούτου κινοῦσα τὰ κατὰ τὸ σῶμα περιττώματα. φθειρῶν τε οὖν πλῆθος οὐκ ὀλίγον γεννᾶται τοῖς ἔχουσι κακοχυμίαν ἐν τοῖς σώμασι δαψιλῆ καὶ τοῦ δέρματος ἀφίσταταί τε καὶ ἀποπίπτει καθάπερ τι λέπος ἡ ἐπιδερμὶς ὀνομαζομένη, καθ’ ἣν ἴσχονται μᾶλλον τῶν ﻿ εἰς τὸ δέρμα φερομένων χυμῶν οἱ παχεῖς καὶ γεώδεις, ὑφ’ ὧν αἵ τε ψῶραι καὶ αἱ λέπραι καὶ οἱ ἐλέφαντες γίνονται. ταῦτα μὲν οὖν εἶχόν σοι λέγειν περὶ τῶν τῆς ἐχίδνης σαρκῶν, ἐφεξῆς δὲ πάλιν ἀναλήψομαι τὸν λόγον. αἱ μὲν τῶν θερμοτέρων φύσει ζώων σάρκες οὐ μόνον τρέφουσιν ἡμᾶς, ἀλλὰ καὶ θερμαίνουσιν, αἱ δὲ τῶν ψυχροτέρων ψύχουσιν. οὕτως δὲ καὶ αἱ μὲν τῶν ξηροτέρων ξηραίνουσιν, αἱ δὲ τῶν ὑγροτέρων ὑγραίνουσιν. μεμνημένος οὖν ὧν ἔμαθες ἐν τοῖς περὶ κράσεων, ὅταν γνωρίσῃς τι τῶν ζώων εἶναι τῇ κράσει ξηρότερον, ὥσπερ εἰ τύχοι τὸν ἄγριον ὗν τοῦ ἡμέρου, γίνωσκε τούτου καὶ τὴν σάρκα ξηραντικωτέραν εἶναι καὶ κατὰ τὰς ἄλλας διαφορὰς τῶν κράσεων ὡσαύτως, οἷον ὅτι συὸς μὲν πρόβατον ξηρότερον, τούτου δ’ αἴξ, τούτου δὲ βοῦς, καὶ τούτου λέων. οὕτω δὲ καὶ κατὰ θερμότητα λέων μὲν κυνὸς θερμότερος, κύων δὲ ταύρου, ταῦρος δὲ τοῦ τοὺς ὄρχεις ἐκτετμημένου βοός. ἀνάλογον οὖν τῇ κατὰ τὴν κρᾶσιν ὑπεροχῇ τῶν ἄλλων ζώων καὶ αἱ σάρκες αὐτῶν διοίσουσιν. καὶ διὰ τοῦτο ξηραίνειν μὲν ἐθέλων τὸ σῶμα τῶν ξηροτέρων τῇ κράσει ζώων δώσεις τὴν σάρκα, θερμαίνειν δὲ βουλόμενος τῶν θερμοτέρων, καὶ ψυχρότερον μὲν ἐπιχειρῶν ἐργάζεσθαι τῶν ψυχροτέρων, ὑγρότερον δὲ τῶν ὑγροτέρων. οὐ σμικρὰ δὲ διαφορὰ τῶν σαρκῶν ἐστι κᾀν τῷ τεταριχεῦσθαί τινας αὐτῶν. ὅλῳ γὰρ παντὶ διαλλάττουσιν, ὡς πολλάκις ὑγροτάτου τῇ κράσει ζώου ταριχευθεῖσαν σάρκα ξηραντικωτέραν εἶναι μακρῷ τῆς φύσεως ξηρᾶς. ἀταριχεύτου καὶ ἡ ὀπτηθεῖσα δὲ ξηροτέρα τῆς ἑψηθείσης ἐστὶν ἐν ὕδατι. ἔγραψαν δὲ καὶ ἄλλων ζώων ἔνιοι σάρκας ὠφελεῖν ἐσθιομένας τε καὶ κατὰ πεπονθότων μερῶν ἐπιτιθεμένας, οἷον τὴν τοῦ χερσαίου ἐχίνου σκελετευθεῖσαν, εἰ ποθείη ἐλεφαντιῶσιν καὶ καχέκταις καὶ σπασμώδεσιν καὶ νεφριτικοῖς, ἔτι τε τοῖς τὸν ἀνασάρκα προσαγορευόμενον ὕδερον ἔχουσιν. εἰ δὲ ταῦτα ποιεῖν πέφυκεν, εἴη ἂν ἡ δύναμις αὐτῆς ἰσχυρῶς διαφορητική τε καὶ ξηραντικὴ, ὥσπερ καὶ ἡ τῆς σκελετευθείσης γαλῆς, ἥπερ οὖν ὀνίνησι τοὺς ἐπιληπτικοὺς πινομένη. τῶν δὲ ταριχευθέντων αἰλούρων τὴν σάρκα λειωθεῖσαν ἐπιτιθεμένην ἐξάγειν σκόλοπάς φασιν, ὡς ἑλκτικὴν ἔχουσαν δύναμιν δηλονότι, τῶν δὲ μυάκων ἁρμόττειν ἕλκεσιν ὑπὸ κυνὸς δάκνοντος γεγονόσιν. ἐγὼ δ’ οὐδεμίαν ἐξαίρετον εὑρίσκω δύναμιν, ἧς δεῖται τὰ τοιαῦτα τῶν ἑλκῶν, ὥσπερ τὰ ὑπὸ τοῦ λυττῶντος κυνὸς δακόντος γενόμενα. καὶ ἡ τῶν κοχλιῶν δὲ σὰρξ κοπεῖσα πρότερον ἐν ὅλμῳ καὶ μετὰ ταῦτα λειωθεῖσα ξηραντικωτάτη γίνεται πάντων τῶν ὑγρότητα περιττὴν ἐχόντων μορίων, ὥστε καὶ τοῖς ὑδερικοῖς ἁρμόττειν. ἡ δὲ ἐξ αὐτῶν ὑγρότης, μόνη καθ’ ἑαυτὴν ἄνευ τῆς σαρκὸς λαμβανομένη, καλεῖται ὑπὸ τῶν πολλῶν μύξα κοχλίου, μιγνυμένη δὲ λιβανωτῷ ἢ ἀλόῃ ἢ σμύρνῃ ἤ τισι τούτων ἢ πᾶσιν ἄχρι τοῦ κηρωτῆς πάχος ἔχειν, ἐχέκολλόν τε γίνεται φάρμακον καὶ ξηραίνει καλῶς τοὺς ὑποπύους μύξους τῶν ὤτων. ἔστι δὲ καὶ ἀνακόλλημα ξηραντικὸν τῶν εἰς ὀφθαλμοὺς ῥευμάτων, ἐπιτιθεμένη κατὰ τὸ μέτωπον. ἔνιοι δὲ καὶ πρὸς σκόλοπας χρῶνται, λειοῦντες ὅλους μετὰ τῶν ὀστράκων, εἰσὶ δ’ οἳ καὶ πρὸς καταμηνίων ἐπίσχεσιν. αὐτὰς δὲ τὰς σάρκας μόνας κᾀγώ ποτε κατ’ ἀγρὸν ἐπὶ τραύματος ἅμα νεύρου τρώσει τε καὶ θλάσει γεγονότος ἐπέθηκα λειώσας, καὶ τό τε τραῦμα καλῶς ἐκολλήθη καὶ τὸ νεῦρον οὐκ ἐφλέγμηνε. ἦν δὲ σκληρὸς καὶ ἀγροῖκος ἄνθρωπος, ἔμιξά γε μὴν λειουμέναις αὐταῖς ἄχνην ἀλεύ ρου, λαβὼν ἀπὸ τοῦ πλησιάζοντος τῇ μύλῃ τοίχου. γεγράφασι δέ τινες τῶν πρὸ ἡμῶν ἰατρῶν σμύρναν ἢ λιβανωτὸν μιγνύειν αὐτοῖς δεῖν ἐπὶ τῆς τοιαύτης χρήσεως, ἀλλ’ οὐδετέραν εἶχον τούτων ἔξωθεν τῆς πόλεως ἐπὶ τῆς ἀγροικίας. δύναιτο δ’ ἄν τις καὶ ῥητίνης φρικτῆς λείας μιγνύειν εἰ παρείη. ὁπόταν μέντοι πολὺ τῆς μύξης τῶν κοχλιῶν ἐθέλεις λαβεῖν μόνης, κατακέντησον αὐτῶν τὴν σάρκα γραφείῳ. χρὴ δὲ μὴ πρὸ πολλῶν ἡμερῶν αὐτοὺς θηρεῦσαι, καταξηραίνονται γὰρ ἐν τῷ χρόνῳ. πρόσφατοι δ’ ὄντες πολὺ τῆς γλίσχρας ὑγρότητος ἔχουσιν, ἣν κατακεντούμενοι τῷ γραφείῳ προϊᾶσιν. ἡ δ’ ὑγρότης αὕτη καὶ τῶν ἐν τοῖς βλεφάροις τριχῶν παρὰ φύσιν ἀνακόλλημα γίνεται.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ὑποπύους μύξους, φρικτῆς, ὀχθῶδες, αὐχμηρὸν, ἐπ' ὄρνισιν
11.1.1 [1. On the flesh of the viper.] Not all the fleshes of animals nourish a human being; those of some are even deadly, no less than the drug-like substances which they call deleterious; and of the fleshes that do nourish us some are this alone, nutriments, while some, in addition to nourishing, also possess the power proper to a drug, since by drying or moistening and heating or cooling they alter the body. At any rate, one can clearly see that those of vipers heat and dry when they are dressed, like eels, with oil and salt and dill and leek and, obviously, water in due measure. That they purge the whole body through the skin you may learn also from what I myself experienced while still a young man in our Asia, each instance of which I shall now go through in order. A man sick with the affection called elephas for a while shared the life of his familiars; but when, from association and intercourse with him, some came to share in the affection, and he himself was by now foul-smelling and hideous to look upon, they built a hut for him near the village on a low part of the hill beside a spring and settled the man in it, bringing him food for as many days as sufficed for him to live on. About the rising of the Dog-star a wine, very fragrant, in an earthenware jar was brought to reapers who were reaping near him. The one who brought it set it down near the reapers and went away; and when the time for drinking came for them, their custom was to pour out a krater and mix the wine with water in due measure, but when a young man had taken up the jar and was pouring the wine out into the krater, a dead viper fell out along with it. So the reapers, fearing lest they suffer some harm from the drink, themselves drank water, and as they were departing they bestowed, out of humanity forsooth, the whole of the wine upon the man sick with elephas, judging it better for him to die than to live in such a state. But he, drinking from it, became healthy in a marvellous manner. For the whole mound-like roughness of his skin fell away, like the covering of the soft-shelled animals; and what remained appeared quite soft, like that of crayfish and crabs when the surrounding shell has fallen off. Another such case came about from a similar chance event in Mysia in Asia, not far from our city. A man suffering with elephas set out to make use of naturally hot waters in hope of benefit. He had a concubine, a slave, young and beautiful, who had many lovers; to her the sick man entrusted certain other matters of the household and indeed also the affairs of the storeroom. When they had lodged, while he was using the waters, in a house that had adjoining to it a parched plot full of vipers, one of these fell into a wine-jar lying carelessly about and died in it; the concubine, considering what had chanced to happen a godsend, offered her master his drink from this jar. And he, drinking it, was healed in the same way as the man in the hut. These, then, are now two lessons for you of experience by chance; and a third in addition to them from our own imitation. For when a certain man sick with this disease, more philosophical than the common run, was terribly distressed and kept saying that it was better to die than to live, to him in so wretched a condition I disclosed the two aforementioned chance occurrences. And he—for he was himself experienced in augury and had the use of a friend who excelled marvellously in that study—sat watching the birds together with him and was prompted to imitate what had been learned through experience, and by drinking wine drugged in this way he became leprous. Some time later we cured his lepra too with the customary drugs. A fourth after this man, who had made it his craft to catch live vipers, was in the beginning of that affection, and it was our concern that he be healed as quickly as possible. So having cut a vein of his and purged him with a drug that evacuates black [bile], we advised him to use the vipers he caught, preparing them in a dish, just like eels. This man, then, was treated in this way, the affection dispersing from him by transpiration. Another man, a wealthy one—this man not a countryman of ours, but he came from the middle of Thrace, a dream having urged him to Pergamon—then, the god having ordered him in a dream both to drink of the drug made from vipers every day and to anoint his body externally with it, the affection changed after not many days into lepra, and this disease too was in turn cured by the drugs the god commanded. So the flesh of vipers reaches this degree of drying power; but since some of those who ate it were seized by a most violent thirst, on this account they also call the vipers dipsades. There are those who say, too, that those bitten by them are not filled when they drink, but burst before they cease to be thirsty. For this reason I inquired of those who hunt vipers in Rome, whom they call Marsi, whether they had any sign to teach me distinguishing each of the two kinds of vipers; but they said there was no kind at all of dipsad vipers, but that those living by the sea and in places having much saline soil have salty flesh, and that for this reason many such arise in Libya, while in Italy there are none because of the moistness of the country. These things, then, I heard the Marsi say, yet I cannot state with certainty whether they tell the truth in the whole or also lie in some respect. For that in the places they mentioned some vipers arise having salty flesh seems to me most plausible, since I know that the fleshes of animals change along with their foods; yet I cannot declare that there is no kind of dipsad vipers. The safest course, therefore, is to guard against hunting the vipers in such places for eating or for the preparation of a drug, such as is this famous one which almost all physicians call theriac. And since it is our custom, when we prepare the so-called theriac troches, to remove not only their head but also the tail, I have often reflected that the whole head is perhaps removed reasonably, on account of the venom contained in the mouth, but the tail unreasonably. For one cannot even say this, that it must be done so on account of the residues of the food, both the moist and the dry. For having killed them, and then skinned and opened them up, we take out and throw away all the inner parts, so that only the substance of the flesh is left along with the arteries and veins that grow through it, which have a very small bulk in relation to the whole flesh and are not even visible unless one examines very carefully. The troches, then, which they indeed also call theriac, we prepare as follows. Taking them cleaned, we then boil them in water until they seem to us thoroughly boiled. We throw in dill along with them into the water right from the start, and after the boiling we separate the spines from the flesh, then mix the flesh with bread ground fine. This bread we take not any chance one, but as far as possible the purest and well baked in a klibanos, having salt and leaven in due measure. We dry it beforehand in a dry room free of damp, until it becomes so dry as to be able to be pounded in a mortar. Yet we do not mix it by pounding, but by soaking it in the water in which we boiled the vipers. The flesh itself, before mixing it with the bread, we grind in a cooks' mortar until it becomes thoroughly smooth. And after this, having moulded small thin troches and then dried them in a warm and dry room, we keep them, putting them away again in such a room. These, then, we prepare at the onset of summer, when the flesh of vipers is at its very best. We use them later, pounding and sifting them, then grinding them smooth again and mixing them with the salts prepared for pleasure, and we also put some of them into the theriac. The salts made from roasted vipers are also made at the same season, we throwing the live vipers into a new pot together with the drugs strewn beneath and placed around them, all of which it is not the business of the present treatise to tell now. For perhaps someone will reasonably reproach us also regarding the preparation of the troches, as having gone through it out of season. But since these things have already been said, let them be kept, even if they seem not very properly to belong to the treatise before us. Let what follows be said in the fitting measure. It is fitting to recall the general power of the flesh of vipers, saying that it is strongly drying and dispersing, together with heating moderately. Its power hastens, as it seems, to the skin, moving through it the residues in the body. So a not small multitude of lice is generated in those who have abundant kakochymia in their bodies, and what is called the epidermis separates from the skin and falls away like a scale, in which the thick and earthy of the humours carried to the skin are retained more, by which psorai and leprai and elephantes come about. These things, then, I had to say to you about the fleshes of the viper; next I shall take up the argument again. The fleshes of animals hotter by nature not only nourish us but also heat, those of colder ones cool. In the same way those of drier ones dry, and those of moister ones moisten. Remembering, then, what you learned in the books On Mixtures, when you recognise some animal to be drier in mixture, as for instance the wild boar than the domestic pig, know that its flesh too is more drying, and likewise for the other differences of mixtures, for example that the sheep is drier than the pig, the goat than this, the ox than this, and the lion than this. So too in heat the lion is hotter than the dog, the dog than the bull, the bull than the ox whose testicles have been cut out. In proportion, then, to the superiority in mixture of the other animals their fleshes too will differ. And on this account, wishing to dry the body you will give the flesh of animals drier in mixture, wishing to heat it, that of hotter ones, attempting to make it colder, that of colder ones, and moister, that of moister ones. Not small, too, is the difference of fleshes in some of them having been salted. For they differ altogether, so that often the salted flesh of an animal moistest in mixture is by far more drying than the unsalted flesh of one dry by nature. And roasted flesh is drier than that boiled in water. Some have also written that the fleshes of other animals help both when eaten and when applied on affected parts, for example that of the land hedgehog dried, if drunk, for sufferers from elephantiasis and cachectics and those subject to spasm and nephritics, and further those who have the dropsy called anasarca. If it is by nature able to do these things, its power would be strongly dispersing and drying, like also that of the dried weasel, which indeed benefits epileptics when drunk. The flesh of salted cats, ground smooth and applied, they say draws out splinters, as evidently having a drawing power, and that of mussels suits wounds that have come about from a biting dog. But I find no special power that such wounds require, as do those arising from the bite of a rabid dog. And the flesh of snails, first pounded in a mortar and afterwards ground smooth, becomes most drying of all parts that have excessive moisture, so that it suits even the dropsical. The moisture from them, taken alone by itself without the flesh, is called by most people snail's slime, and mixed with frankincense or aloe or myrrh or some of these or all, until it has the thickness of a cerate, it becomes a glue-holding drug and dries well the suppurating slimy discharges (ὑποπύους μύξους) of the ears. It is also a drying anakollema for fluxes into the eyes, applied on the forehead. Some use it also for splinters, grinding them whole together with their shells, and there are some who use it also for the checking of the menses. The fleshes alone by themselves I too once in the country applied, having ground them smooth, upon a wound that had come about together with a wounding and crushing of a sinew, and the wound was well glued together and the sinew did not become inflamed. He was a hard and rustic man; I did however mix with them, as they were being ground, flour-dust taken from the wall adjoining the mill. Some of the physicians before us have written that myrrh or frankincense must be mixed with them for such use, but I had neither of these outside the city on the farm. One could also mix in smooth roasted (φρικτῆς) resin, if it were at hand. When, however, you wish to take much of the slime of the snails alone, prick their flesh with a stylus. They must not have been caught many days before, for they dry up with time. When fresh they have much of the viscous moisture, which they emit when pricked with the stylus. This moisture also becomes an anakollema for the hairs in the eyelids that are contrary to nature.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὀχθῶδες, ἀταριχεύτου (punctuation corrupt; taken with the preceding clause), μυάκων, φρικτῆς (read as φρυκτῆς, 'roasted'), ἀνακόλλημα, διαπνεύσαντος, καθίσας ἐπ’ ὄρνισιν, ἀρτυομένας
11.1.1 [1. On the flesh of the viper.] Not all the fleshes of animals nourish a human being. Those of some are even deadly, no less than the drug-like substances which they call dēlētēria. Of the fleshes that do nourish us, some are foods and only that, while others, besides nourishing, also have the power as of a drug, since they alter the body by drying or moistening and by heating or cooling. At any rate, one can see plainly that the fleshes of vipers heat and dry when they are dressed as eels are, with oil, salts, dill, leek, and of course water in due measure. That they cleanse the whole body through the skin you may learn also from what I experienced when I was still young, in our Asia; I shall now go through each of these cases in order. A man ill with the affection called elephas for a time shared the same regimen with his household. But when, from association and intercourse with him, some of them came to share the affection, and he himself was by now foul-smelling and loathsome to look upon, they built a hut for him near the village on a low hill beside a spring and settled the man in it, bringing him each day as much food as was sufficient to live on. About the rising of the Dog Star, some wine was brought in an earthenware jar to reapers who were reaping near him; it was very fragrant. The man who brought it set it down near the reapers and went away. When the time for drinking came, their custom was to pour the wine out into a mixing-bowl and mix it with a moderate amount of water. But when a young man took up the jar and was drawing the wine out into the mixing-bowl, a dead viper fell out along with it. The reapers, fearing that they might suffer some harm from the drink, drank water themselves. As they were leaving, they made a present of the whole wine, ostensibly out of humanity, to the man ill with elephas, judging that it was better for him to be dead than to live in such a state. He, drinking from it, became healthy in a wonderful manner. For the whole lumpy part of his skin fell off like the covering of the soft-shelled animals, and what remained appeared quite soft, like that of crayfish and crabs when the shell around them has fallen off. Another such case arose from a similar chance occurrence in Asian Mysia, not far from our city. A man suffering from elephas set out to use naturally hot waters in the hope of benefit. He had a concubine, a slave, young and beautiful and with many lovers. To her the sick man entrusted various other affairs of the household, and in particular those of the storeroom. While he was using the waters they lodged in a house that had next to it a dry, squalid plot full of vipers. One of the vipers fell into a jar of wine that had been carelessly left lying and died in it. The concubine, considering this chance event a windfall, offered her master his drink from that jar. He, drinking it, was healed in the same way as the man in the hut. These two lessons you now have from the experience of chance occurrence; and besides them a third, from our imitation. For a man ill with this disease, more philosophical than the many, was terribly vexed by it and kept saying that it was better to be dead than to live. Since he was in so wretched a condition, I disclosed to him the two chance occurrences just related. He was himself experienced in bird-omens and had a friend wonderfully successful in this learning. Taking his seat with that friend to observe the birds, he was urged on to imitate what had been learned through experience, and by drinking wine drugged in this way he became leprous. Some time later we cured his lepra too with the usual drugs. A fourth man after this, who had made it his craft to catch live vipers, was at the beginning of that affection, and our purpose was that he should be cured as quickly as possible. So, having cut his vein and purged him with a drug that evacuates black bile, we advised him to make use of the vipers he caught, preparing them in a lopas as one does eels. This man, then, was treated in this way, the affection being breathed out of him through transpiration. Another man, a rich one, was not of our country but came from the middle of Thrace, a dream having urged him to Pergamum. The god then commanded him in a dream to drink each day of the drug made from vipers and to anoint his body from outside. Not many days later the affection changed into lepra, and this disease too was cured in its turn with the drugs the god ordered. So great a degree of drying power, then, does the flesh of vipers attain. But some of those who ate it were seized by a most violent thirst, and for this reason they call the vipers dipsades. There are also some who say that those bitten by them, though they drink, are not filled, but burst before they cease to thirst. For this reason I inquired of those in Rome who hunt vipers, whom they call Marsi, whether they had any sign to teach me that distinguishes the two kinds of vipers. They said that there is no kind of dipsas vipers at all. Rather, those that live by the sea and in places with much saline earth (halmyris) have briny flesh, and for this reason many such occur in Libya, but there are none in Italy because of the moistness of the land. These things, then, I heard the Marsi say, but I cannot state with certainty whether they speak the truth in everything or are also lying in some respect. That in the places they named some vipers occur which have salty flesh seems to me most plausible, for I know that the fleshes of animals change along with their foods. But I cannot declare that there is no kind of dipsas vipers. The safest course, then, is to take care not to hunt vipers in such places, whether for eating or for the preparation of a drug, such as this celebrated one which nearly all physicians call theriac. Now it is our custom, when we prepare the so-called theriac pastilles (artiskoi), to remove not only their head but also the tail. I have often reflected that the whole head is perhaps reasonably removed, on account of the venom contained in the mouth, but the tail unreasonably. Nor can one say that it must be done so on account of the residues of the food, both the moist and the dry. For having killed them, then flayed them and laid them open, we take out and throw away all the inner parts, so that only the substance of the fleshes is left, together with the arteries and veins that grow through it. These have a very small bulk relative to the whole flesh and are not even visible unless one inspects very carefully. The pastilles, then, which they also name theriac pastilles, we prepare thus. Taking the vipers cleaned, we boil them in water until they seem to us to be exactly boiled. We put dill into the water along with them right from the start. After the boiling we separate the spines from the fleshes, and then mix the flesh with ground bread. This bread we do not take at random, but the purest possible, well baked in an oven (klibanos), with a moderate amount of salt and leaven. We dry it beforehand in a dry room without moisture, until it becomes dry enough to be pounded in a mortar. Yet we do not mix it in by pounding it, but by soaking it in the water in which we boiled the vipers. The flesh itself, before mixing it with the bread, we triturate in a cooks' mortar until it becomes exactly smooth. After this, having moulded small thin pastilles, we dry them in a warm and dry room and keep them, storing them again in such a room. These we prepare at the onset of summer, when the flesh of vipers is at its very best. Later we use them by pounding and sifting them, then triturating them again and mixing them with salts prepared for pleasantness of taste; we also put them into the theriac. At the same season the salts made from roasted vipers are also prepared. We throw the vipers alive into a new pot together with the drugs strewn underneath and laid around them, all of which it is not the business of the present treatise to state now. Indeed, someone might perhaps reasonably reproach us even over the preparation of the pastilles, as having gone through it out of place. But since these things have already been said, let them stand, even if they seem not altogether proper to the present treatise. What follows, let it be said in fitting measure. It is fitting to recall, concerning the general power of the flesh of vipers, that it is drying and strongly dispersing, while heating moderately. Its power, it seems, presses towards the skin, and through the skin it moves the residues in the body. So a not inconsiderable multitude of lice is generated in those who have abundant kakochymia in their bodies. Also, the so-called epidermis separates from the skin and falls off like a husk; it is in the epidermis that the thick and earthy humours, among those carried to the skin, are more held back, and by these psōrai, leprai and elephantes arise. This, then, is what I had to tell you about the fleshes of the viper; next I shall take up the account again. The fleshes of animals that are hotter by nature not only nourish us but also heat, while those of colder animals cool. In the same way, those of drier animals dry, and those of moister animals moisten. Remember, then, what you learned in the work On Mixtures. When you recognise that some animal is drier in mixture, as for instance the wild boar than the domestic pig, know that its flesh too is more drying. The same holds for the other differences of mixtures: for example, the sheep is drier than the pig, the goat than the sheep, the ox than the goat, and the lion than the ox. So too in respect of heat, the lion is hotter than the dog, the dog than the bull, and the bull than the ox whose testicles have been cut out. In proportion, then, to their excess in mixture over the other animals, their fleshes will also differ. For this reason, when you wish to dry the body, you will give the flesh of animals drier in mixture; when you want to heat it, that of hotter ones; when you undertake to make it colder, that of colder ones; and when moister, that of moister ones. No small difference among the fleshes lies also in some of them having been salted. For they differ altogether, so that often the salted flesh of an animal most moist in mixture is far more drying than the unsalted flesh of one dry by nature. And the roasted flesh is drier than that boiled in water. Some have also written that the fleshes of other animals benefit when eaten and when applied to the affected parts. One example is the flesh of the land hedgehog, dried, if it is drunk by those with elephantiasis, cachectics, those subject to spasms and nephritics, and further by those who have the dropsy called anasarca. If it is of a nature to do these things, its power would be strongly dispersing and drying, as is that of the dried weasel, which indeed benefits epileptics when drunk. They say that the flesh of salted cats, ground and applied, draws out splinters, evidently as having a drawing power. They also say that the flesh of mussels (myakes) suits ulcers caused by a biting dog. I, however, find no special power that ulcers of this kind require, as those produced by the bite of a rabid dog do. The flesh of snails too, first pounded in a mortar and afterwards triturated, becomes the most drying of all for parts having superfluous moisture, so that it suits the dropsical as well. The moisture from them, taken alone by itself without the flesh, is called by most people snail's mucus. Mixed with frankincense or aloe or myrrh, or some or all of these, until it has the thickness of a cerate, it becomes a glue-like drug and dries the purulent mucous discharges of the ears well. Applied on the forehead, it is also a drying adhesive application (anakollēma) against fluxes into the eyes. Some also use the snails for splinters, triturating them whole with their shells, and there are some who use them for checking the menses. The fleshes alone I myself once triturated and applied, in the country, to a wound that had occurred together with a wounding and crushing of a nerve. The wound was glued together well and the nerve did not become inflamed. The man was tough and rustic; I mixed with the fleshes, as they were being triturated, fine flour-dust, taking it from the wall next to the mill. Some of the physicians before us have written that one ought to mix myrrh or frankincense with them for such a use, but I had neither of these, being outside the city on the country estate. One could also mix in ground roasted resin, if it is to hand. Whenever you wish to obtain much of the snails' mucus by itself, prick their flesh with a stylus. One must not catch them many days beforehand, for they dry out over time. When fresh they have much of the viscous moisture, which they emit when pricked with the stylus. This moisture also serves to glue back the hairs in the eyelids that are contrary to nature.

Greek (Kühn)
11.1.2 [β΄. Περὶ πιμελῆς καὶ στέατος.] Πιμελὴ καὶ στέαρ. κοινότατα καὶ ταῦτα τῶν ἐν τοῖς ζώοις μορίων ἐστί. τὰ γὰρ εὐτροφοῦντα πάντα πιμελὴν ἢ στέαρ ἴσχει, τὰ δὲ ἰσχνὰ καὶ ἄτροφα πολλάκις μὲν οὐδ’ ὅλως ἔχειν φαίνεται, πολλάκις δ’ οὕτως ὀλίγην τε καὶ ξηρὰν, ὡς δύσχρηστον εἶναι. διαφέρει δὲ πιμελῆς στέαρ τῷ πάχει, διὸ καὶ τοῖς γεωδεστέραν ἔχουσιν τὴν ὅλην φύσιν ζώοις τὸ στέαρ γίνεται καθάπερ τοῖς ὑγροτέραν ἡ πιμελή. διὰ τοῦτο τοιγαροῦν ἡ μὲν πιμελὴ τήκεται ῥαδίως ὑπὸ τοῦ πυρὸς, οὐ ῥᾳδίως δὲ πήγνυται τακεῖσα. τὸ δὲ στέαρ οὔτε τήκεται ῥᾳδίως, κᾂν τακῇ, πήγνυται τάχιστα καὶ φαίνεται πολὺ σκληρότερον τῆς πιμελῆς. ὗς μὲν οὖν εὐτροφῶν πιμελὴν πλείστην ἔχει διὰ τὴν φυσικὴν τῆς κράσεως ὑγρότητα, βόες δὲ καὶ αἶγες ὅσα τε ἄλλα κερασφόρα διὰ τὴν ξηρότητα τὸ στέαρ πλεῖστον γεννᾷ. σὺ δ’ εἰ βούλει τὴν ἐλαιώδη καὶ λιπαρὰν οὐσίαν ἅπασαν ἐν τοῖς ζώοις κάλει στέαρ, ὥσπερ οἱ πλεῖστοι τῶν ἰατρῶν. ἔξεστι δέ σοι καὶ πιμελὴν ὀνομάζειν ἅπαν τοῦτο τὸ γένος. οὐ μὴν ἔξεστί γέ σοι βουλομένῳ μὴ ψεύδεσθαι τὸ στέαρ τῶν αἰγῶν ὑγρότερον εἶναι φάναι τοῦ τῶν ὑῶν. ὑγρότατον γὰρ τοῦτο σχεδὸν ἁπάντων τῶν ἄλλων ἐστὶ, καὶ ﻿ διὰ τοῦτο κατὰ τὴν ἐνέργειαν ἐγγὺς ἐλαίου τὴν δύναμιν, μαλακτικώτερόν γε μὴν ἐλαίου καὶ πεπτικώτερόν ἐστι. καὶ διὰ τοῦτο τοῖς πρὸς φλεγμονὰς ἁρμόττουσιν καταπλάσμασι μίγνυται. τοῖς δὲ δακνομένοις τὰ κατὰ τὸ ἀπευθυσμένον ἢ τὸ κῶλον ἐνίεμεν αἴγειον μᾶλλον ἢ ὕειον στέαρ, οὐχ ὅτι μᾶλλον ἀμβλυτικόν ἐστι δριμύτητος, ὅσον γὰρ ἐπὶ τῇ φύσει μᾶλλον ἀμβλύνει τὸ ὕειον καὶ διὰ τοῦτο μίγνυται καὶ τοῖς ἕλκη θεραπεύουσι φαρμάκοις, οἷόν πέρ ἐστι καὶ τὸ καλούμενον πάρυγρον. ἀλλ’ ὅτι τὸ μὲν αἴγειον πήγνυται ῥᾳδίως διὰ πάχος, ἀποῤῥεῖ δὲ τὸ ὕειον ὁμοίως τῷ ἐλαίῳ διὰ τοῦτ’ ἐνίεμεν αὐτὸ μᾶλλον ἐπὶ τῶν δυσεντερικῶν τε καὶ τεινεσμωδῶν, ὅταν παρηγορῆσαι τὴν δῆξιν αὐτῶν βουληθῶμεν. καὶ διὰ λεπτομέρειαν δ’ ἔνια μᾶλλον ἀμβλύνει δριμύτητας, ὅταν ἐν τῷ βάθει τῶν δακνομένων σωμάτων τὸ λυποῦν ᾖ. ἧττον μὲν γὰρ τὸ παχυμερὲς, μᾶλλον δὲ τὸ λεπτομερὲς ὅλα διεξέρχεται τὰ σώματα καὶ πᾶσι κεράννυται τοῖς δάκνουσιν ἰχῶρσιν αὐτό. κατὰ τοῦτο γοῦν καὶ τὸ χήνειον στέαρ τὰ κατὰ βάθος τῶν σωμάτων δάκνοντα μᾶλλον ἀμβλύνει. ἔστι δὲ καὶ θερμότερον τοῦ ὑείου. μεταξύ γε μὴν αὐτῶν ἐστι τὸ τῶν ἀλεκτρυόνων, θερμότερον δ’ ἀεὶ καὶ ξηρότερον τὸ τῶν ἀῤῥένων ζώων ἐστίν. αὐτῶν δὲ τούτων τὸ τῶν ἐκτεμνομένων τοὺς ὄρχεις ἧττον θερμόν τε καὶ ξηρόν. ὁμοιοῦται γὰρ ἀεὶ τὸ εὐνουχισθὲν ἄῤῥεν τοῖς ὁμογενέσι θήλεσι. κεφάλαιον δ’ ἐστὶ τοῦ λόγου τόδε κοινὸν, οὗ μεμνῆσθαι χρὴ, τὸ κατὰ τὴν τῶν ζώων κρᾶσιν ἀεὶ τὰς διαφορὰς γίνεσθαι τῆς ἐν αὐτοῖς πιμελῆς ἢ στέατος ἢ ὅπως ἂν ὀνομάζειν ἐθέλῃς ὅλον τὸ γένος τοῦτο τοῦ κατὰ τὰ ζῶα σώματος ἐλαιώδους τε καὶ λιπαροῦ. ἐπεὶ τοίνυν ὗς ἀπολείπεται κατὰ θερμότητά τε καὶ ξηρότητα σχεδὸν ἁπάντων τῶν τετραπόδων ζώων, διὰ τοῦτο καὶ πιμελὴν ἧττόν τε θερμὴν ἔχει καὶ μᾶλλον ὑγράν. ἁπάσης μὲν οὖν πιμελῆς δύναμίς ἐστιν ὑγραντική τε καὶ θερμαντικὴ τῶν ἀνθρωπίνων σωμάτων. τούτου γὰρ μεμνῆσθαι χρὴ διὰ παντὸς, εἰρημένου κατὰ τὴν ἀρχὴν τῆσδε τῆς πραγματείας· αἱ δὲ καθ’ ἕκαστον αὐτῶν διαφοραὶ κατὰ τὸ μᾶλλόν τε καὶ ἧττόν εἰσιν. τοῦ μὲν γὰρ ὑὸς ὡς πρὸς ἡμᾶς ἡ πιμελὴ τὸ μὲν ὑγραίνειν ἔχει δαψιλῶς, τὸ θερμαίνειν δ’ οὐχ ὁμοίως ὥσπερ τοὔλαιον, ἀλλ’ ἔστι κατὰ τὴν ἐν ἡμῖν θερμότητα καὶ ἡ τοῦ λίπους τῶν ὑῶν. ἡ δὲ τῶν ταύρων πολὺ θερμοτέρα καὶ ξηροτέρα τῆς τῶν ὑῶν, μεμνημένων ἡμῶν πάλιν κἀνταῦθα τὸ μὲν ἄῤῥεν τοῦ θήλεος ἀεὶ ξηρότερον εἶναι καὶ θερμότερον, τὸ δ’ εὐνουχισθὲν ἄῤῥεν ὁμοιοῦσθαι τῷ θήλει, καθάπερ γε καὶ τὸ νέον ἅπαν. αὐτῶν δὲ τῶν νέων τὸ θῆλυ τοῦ ἄῤῥενος ὑγρότερόν τε εἶναι καὶ ἧττον θερμόν. οὕτως οὖν καὶ τὸ μόσχειον στέαρ ἀπολείπεται τοῦ τῶν ταύρων θερμότητί τε καὶ ξηρότητι καὶ τὸ τῶν ἐρίφων τοῦ τῶν αἰγῶν, ἀπολείπεται δὲ καὶ τὸ τῶν αἰγῶν τοῦ τῶν τράγων, αὐτῶν δὴ πάλιν τὸ τῶν ταύρων τοῦ τῶν λεόντων· διαφορητικώτατον γὰρ ἐκεῖνο σχεδὸν ἁπάντων τῶν ἐν τοῖς τετράποσι. καὶ γὰρ καὶ θερμότατον αὐτῶν ἐστι καὶ λεπτομερέστατον, ὥστε εἰ μίξαις αὐτὸ τοῖς πρὸς ἕλκη καὶ φλεγμονὰς ἁρμόττουσι φαρμάκοις, οὐ μόνον οὐδὲν ὀνήσεις, ἀλλὰ καὶ βλάψεις δριμύτερον τοῦ προσήκοντος ἐργασάμενος. ὄγκοις δὲ χρονίοις ἐσκληρυσμένοις καὶ νεύρων ἀγκύλαις καὶ ἁπλῶς ὅσα σκιῤῥώδη, προσφορώτατόν ἐστιν ἐφ’ ὧν ἀπρακτότατον φαίνεται τὸ τῶν ὑῶν. τὸ δὲ τῶν ταύρων ἶσόν πως ἑκατέρου διέστηκεν, ὅσῳ θερμότερόν τέ ἐστι καὶ ξηρότερον τοῦ τῶν ὑῶν, τοσούτῳ καὶ τοῦ τῶν λεόντων ἀπολειπόμενον. ὡς ἂν οὖν ἐν τῷ μέσῳ τεταγμένον, εἰκότως ἑκατέρῳ τῷ γένει μίγνυται τῶν φαρμάκων, τοῖς τε τὰ σκιῤῥώδη θεραπεύουσι. καὶ τοῖς τὰ φλεγμαίνοντα πέπτουσιν, ὁποῖόν ἐστιν τὸ τετραφάρμακον ὀνομαζόμενον ἐκ κηροῦ καὶ ῥητίνης καὶ πίττης καὶ στέατος συγκειμένου. εἰς γάρ τοι τοῦτο, κᾂν ταύρειον βάλῃς κᾂν μόσχειον κᾂν τράγειον κᾂν αἴγειον κᾂν ὕειον, ἐκπυπτικόν τε καὶ πεπτικὸν ἐργάσῃ φάρμακον. ἀλλὰ τὸ μὲν ὕειον ἐμβληθὲν ἐπὶ παίδων ἁρμόζει καὶ γυναικῶν καὶ συνελόντι φάναι τῶν ἁπαλοσάρκων, τὸ δὲ τοῦ ταύρου σκαπανεῦσι καὶ θερισταῖς καὶ πᾶσι τοῖς σκληρὰς ἔχουσι σάρκας ἢ διὰ τὴν φυσικὴν κρᾶσιν ἢ διὰ τὴν ἰδέαν τοῦ βίου χρήσιμον ὑπάρχει. χρονίζον δ’ ἅπαν στέαρ ἑαυτοῦ γίνεται θερμότερόν τε καὶ λεπτομερέστερον, ὥστε καὶ διαφορητικώτερον. ὑπάρχει δὲ τοῦτο σχεδὸν ἅπασι τοῖς χρονίζουσιν, ὅσα γε μὴ φθάνει σαπῆναι. καὶ γὰρ οἶνος καὶ μέλι καὶ ὄξος καὶ τυρὸς καὶ βούτυρον καὶ οἴσυπος ἔλαιόν τε πᾶν, εἴτε σχίνινον εἴτε κίκινον εἴτε ῥαφάνινον εἴτ’ αὐτὸ τὸ ἐκ τῶν ἐλαιῶν εἴη, χρονίζοντα λεπτότερά τε καὶ λεπτομερέστερα γίνεται καὶ διὰ τοῦτο δριμύτερά τε γευομένοις φαίνεται διαφορητικώτερόν τε χρωμένοις, ἐπὶ τῶν σκληρυνομένων τε καὶ δυσλύτων ἁπάντων παθῶν. ἐγὼ μὲν οὖν εἴρηκα δριμύτερον γευομένοις φαίνεσθαι. τινὲς δὲ τῶν διαφθειρόντων τὰ σημαινόμενα τῶν ὀνομάτων οὐ δριμέα καλοῦσιν, ἀλλὰ στύφοντα τὰ τοιαῦτα πάντα, μέχρι τοῦ πεπέρεως, ὡς οὐδὲν διαφέρον ἢ στῦφον εἰπεῖν ἢ δριμύ. καὶ εἰ αὖθις αὐτοὺς ἔροιο περί τε κηκίδων καὶ μύρτων καὶ μεσπίλων καὶ σιδίων ῥοιᾶς, ὀμφακίου τε καὶ ῥοῦ, καὶ ταῦτα στύφειν φασὶ, καίτοι γε ἐναντιωτάτην αἴσθησιν ἔχομεν ἐπ’ αὐτῶν τῆς γινομένης ἡμῖν ἀπὸ πεπέρεως καὶ πυρέθρου καὶ νάπυος, εὐφορβίου τε καὶ κρομμύου καὶ σκορόδου καὶ ἀδάρκης. εἰ μὲν οὖν ὡς τὸ τῆς στύψεως ὄνομα κοινὸν ἐπιφέρουσιν, κατ’ ἀμφοτέρων τῶν γιγνομένων ἡμῖν ἐν τῇ γεύσει παθῶν, οὕτω καὶ αὐτὰ τὰ ﻿ πάθη μίαν ἔχειν οἴονται φύσιν, ἀλλ’ ὅτι μᾶλλον ἢ ἀνθρώπους αὐτοὺς εἶναι νομιστέον ἰδίας αἰσθήσεις ἔχοντας μόνους. εἰ δ’ ὄμφακα μὲν καὶ ῥοῦν καὶ κηκῖδα βαλαύστιόν τε καὶ ὑποκυστίδα, συνάγειν τε καὶ σφίγγειν ἡμῶν ὁμολογοῦσι τὴν οὐσίαν, διακρίνειν τε καὶ δάκνειν καὶ διεξέρχεσθαι τὸ πέπερι καὶ νᾶπυ καὶ πύρεθρον, ἀκύρως μὲν ὀνομάζουσι στύφοντα τὰ δάκνοντα καὶ θερμαίνοντα, τῆς γνώσεως μέντοι τῶν φαρμάκων αὐτῶν οὐχ ἡμαρτήκασιν. εἰ δὲ δεῖ στοχασάμενον εἰπεῖν πότερον ἐν τοῖς ὀνόμασιν ἢ ἐν τοῖς πράγμασι σφάλλονται, φαίην ἂν ἔγωγε μᾶλλον ἐν τοῖς ὀνόμασιν ἐσφάλθαι τοὺς ἄνδρας, καὶ μάλιστα τοὺς ἀήθεις Ἑλλάδος φωνῆς, οἷός ἐστιν ὁ Ἀναζαρβεὺς Διοσκορίδης, πολλὰ μὲν καλῶς εἴρηκε τῶν περὶ τῆς ἰατρικῆς ὕλης ἱστορουμένων, ἀγνοῶν δὲ τὰ σημαινόμενα τῶν Ἑλληνικῶν ὀνομάτων. οὗτος οὖν, ὅταν λέγει στυπτικώτερον στέαρ εἶναι τὸ τῶν αἰγῶν τοῦ τῶν ὑῶν, εἰ μὲν οὖν τὸ δριμύτερον διὰ τοῦ στυπτικωτέρου βούλεται δηλῶσαι, ἐκδεξόμεθα τὸν λόγον ὡς ἀληθῆ· εἰ δὲ τὸ τοιαύτην ἔχον ποιότητά τε καὶ δύναμιν, οἷον ῥοῦς καὶ ῥῆον ὑπόκυστίς τε καὶ βαλαύστιον, οὐκ ἀληθῆ φήσομεν εἶναι τὸν λόγον. ἐπειδὴ δὲ καὶ τὸ τῶν ἐχιδνῶν στέαρ, ὅταν ἐκ ῥιζῶν τις ἀνασπάσας τὰς ἐν ταῖς μασχάλαις τρίχας ἐπαλείφῃ, φασὶ μὴ καὶ ταύτας φύεσθαι, κάλλιον ἔδοξέ μοι καὶ τούτου πειραθῆναι, καὶ πράξας ὡς ἐκέλευσαν εὗρον ψευδομένους αὐτοὺς, ὥσπερ καὶ ὅτι τὰς ἀρχὰς τῶν ὑποχύσεων ἰᾶται. τὸ δὲ τῶν ἄρκτων στέαρ ἀλωπεκίαις ἁρμόττείν ἀληθῶς ἔγραψαν, ἔχομεν δ’ αὐτοῦ δοκιμώτερα φάρμακα πρὸς τὸ πάθος. ἀλλὰ τό γε μὴν τῶν ἀλωπέκων ὅταν ὠταλγίας φῶσι θεραπεύειν, ἄνευ τοῦ διορίσασθαι ποίας λέγουσιν ὠταλγίας, ὡς οὐδὲ γινώσκουσιν διηρθρωμένως αὐτοῖς ἃ λέγουσιν, οὐδ’ ὅλως χρὴ προσέχειν τὸν νοῦν. ἄλλοι δὲ ἰχθύων ἐπαινοῦσι στέαρ, ὡς πρὸς τὰς ὑποχύσεις ἢ ὅπερ ἂν ἐθέλωσι πάθος ὀνομάζειν. ἕτεροι δ’ οὐχ ἁπλῶς ἰχθύων, ἀλλὰ ποταμίων γράφουσιν, ἄλλοι δὲ μᾶλλον θαλαττίων, ἵνα δοκῶσί τι πλέον ἐπίστασθαι τῶν πολλῶν.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐκπυπτικόν, ἀλλ’ ὅτι μᾶλλον ἢ ἀνθρώπους, ἀδάρκης, πάρυγρον, ῥῆον, οἴσυπος, ἀγκύλαις, ὑποχύσεων
11.1.2 [2. On soft fat and hard fat.] Soft fat and hard fat. These too are among the most common of the parts in animals. For all well-nourished animals have soft fat or hard fat, whereas the lean and ill-nourished often appear to have none at all, and often so little and so dry that it is difficult to use. Hard fat differs from soft fat in thickness; and hence hard fat arises in animals whose whole nature is more earthy, just as soft fat arises in those whose nature is moister. For this reason, accordingly, soft fat melts easily under fire but, once melted, does not easily congeal; hard fat, on the other hand, neither melts easily, and if it is melted it congeals very quickly and appears much harder than soft fat. A well-nourished pig, then, has the most soft fat, owing to the natural moistness of its mixture, whereas cattle and goats and all the other horned animals, owing to their dryness, produce the most hard fat. But you, if you wish, may call the whole oily and fatty substance in animals hard fat, as most physicians do; and you are also permitted to name this whole genus soft fat. What you are not permitted to do, if you wish not to speak falsely, is to say that the fat of goats is moister than that of pigs. For this latter is nearly the moistest of all the others, and for this reason it is in its activity close to oil in power, though it is more softening than oil and more concocting. And for this reason it is mixed into the poultices suited to inflammations. But for those who suffer biting in the region of the rectum or the colon we inject goat's rather than pig's fat, not because it is more blunting of acridity (for so far as nature is concerned pig's fat blunts more, and for this reason it is mixed also into the drugs that treat ulcers, such as the one called parhygron), but because goat's fat congeals easily on account of its thickness, whereas pig's fat flows away like oil; for this reason we inject the former rather in cases of dysentery and tenesmus, when we wish to soothe their biting. And some, through fineness of particles, blunt acridities more, when the painful agent is in the depth of the bodies that are being bitten. For the coarse-particled passes through whole bodies less, the fine-particled more, and it is mingled with all the biting ichors. In this respect, accordingly, goose fat too blunts more what bites in the depth of bodies. It is also hotter than pig's fat. Between them, indeed, is that of cocks; and that of male animals is always hotter and drier. And among these themselves, that of those whose testicles have been excised is less hot and dry; for the castrated male is always assimilated to the females of the same kind. The common head of the argument, which must be kept in mind, is this: that the differences of the soft fat or hard fat in animals, or however you wish to name this whole genus of the oily and fatty part of the body in animals, always arise according to the mixture of the animals. Since, then, the pig falls short in heat and dryness of nearly all four-footed animals, for this reason it also has soft fat that is less hot and more moist. Now the power of all soft fat is moistening and heating of human bodies; for this must be kept in mind throughout, having been stated at the beginning of this treatise; but the differences among each of them are according to more and less. For the soft fat of the pig, relative to us, possesses moistening abundantly, but heating not in the same way as oil does; rather, the fat of pigs too is at the level of the heat in us. That of bulls is much hotter and drier than that of pigs, we remembering again here too that the male is always drier and hotter than the female, and that the castrated male is assimilated to the female, as indeed is everything young; and that among the young themselves the female is moister and less hot than the male. Thus, then, calf's fat also falls short of that of bulls in heat and dryness, and that of kids of that of goats; and that of she-goats falls short of that of he-goats, and among these again that of bulls of that of lions; for the latter is the most dispersing of nearly all the fats in four-footed animals. For it is also the hottest of them and the most fine-particled, so that if you were to mix it into the drugs suited to ulcers and inflammations, not only will you do no good but you will also do harm, having made the drug more pungent than is fitting. But for chronic hardened swellings and contractures of sinews and, in general, all scirrhous conditions it is most serviceable, cases in which pig's fat appears most ineffective. That of bulls stands roughly equally distant from each: by as much as it is hotter and drier than that of pigs, by so much it falls short of that of lions. Since, then, it is set in the middle, it is reasonably mixed into both kinds of drugs, both those that treat scirrhous conditions and those that concoct inflamed parts, such as the so-called tetrapharmakon, composed of wax and resin and pitch and fat. For into this, whether you put bull's or calf's or he-goat's or she-goat's or pig's fat, you will make a drug that brings to suppuration and concocts. But pig's fat, when put in, is suitable for children and women and, in a word, the soft-fleshed, while that of the bull is useful for diggers and reapers and all who have hard flesh either through natural mixture or through the kind of life they lead. All fat, as it ages, becomes hotter than it was and more fine-particled, and so also more dispersing. This holds for nearly all things that age, those at least that do not first putrefy. For wine and honey and vinegar and cheese and butter and oisypos and every oil, whether mastic oil or castor oil or radish-seed oil or the oil from olives itself, when aged become thinner and more fine-particled, and for this reason appear more pungent to those who taste them and more dispersing to those who use them, in all conditions that are hardening and difficult to dissolve. Now I have said that it appears more pungent to those who taste it. But some of those who corrupt the meanings of words call all such things not pungent but astringent, as far as pepper, as though it made no difference to say astringent or pungent. And if you were to ask them again about oak-galls and myrtle-berries and medlars and pomegranate rind, and omphakion and sumac, they say that these too are astringent, although we have in their case a sensation most opposite to that which arises in us from pepper and pyrethron and mustard, and euphorbion and onion and garlic and adarke. If, then, just as they apply the name of astringency in common to both of the affections that arise in us in tasting, so they also think that the affections themselves have a single nature, then one must suppose them to be something other than human beings, alone possessing senses of their own. But if they agree that the unripe grape and sumac and oak-gall and balaustion and hypokistis draw together and constrict our substance, whereas pepper and mustard and pyrethron separate and bite and pass through, then they name improperly as astringent the things that bite and heat, yet in the knowledge of the drugs themselves they have not gone wrong. And if one must say by conjecture whether they go wrong in names or in things, I for my part would say that the men have gone wrong rather in names, and especially those unaccustomed to the Greek language, such as Dioscorides of Anazarbus, who has said many things well among the matters recorded concerning medical material, but is ignorant of the meanings of Greek words. This man, then, when he says that the fat of goats is more astringent than that of pigs: if by more astringent he wishes to indicate more pungent, we shall accept the statement as true; but if he means that which has such a quality and power as sumac and rhubarb and hypokistis and balaustion, we shall say that the statement is not true. And since they say that the fat of vipers, too, when someone has plucked out from the roots the hairs in the armpits and smears it on, prevents these too from growing, it seemed to me better to test this as well, and having done as they bade I found them to be lying, just as also in their claim that it cures the beginnings of cataracts. That bear's fat is suited to alopecia they wrote truly, but we have drugs more approved than it for the condition. But when they say that fox's fat cures ear-aches, without distinguishing which ear-aches they mean, since they do not even know in an articulated way what they are saying, one must not pay attention at all. Others praise the fat of fishes, as for cataracts or whatever condition they wish to name. Others write not simply of fishes but of river fish, others rather of sea fish, so that they may seem to know something more than the many.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ἀλλ’ ὅτι (probably corrupt for ἄλλο τι, rendered 'anything rather than'), ἐκπυπτικόν (taken as ἐκπυητικόν, 'suppurative'), ῥῆον (rhēon; possibly a corruption, or rhubarb), ὀμφακίου (omphakion, unripe-grape juice), πάρυγρον (name of a drug), οἴσυπος (oesypum, wool-grease)
11.1.2 [2. On fat and suet.] Fat (pimelē) and suet (stear). These too are among the commonest of the parts in animals. For all well-nourished animals have fat or suet, whereas lean and ill-nourished ones often appear to have none at all, and often have so little, and that so dry, that it is hard to use. Suet differs from fat in thickness; for this reason suet arises in animals whose whole nature is more earthy, just as fat does in those whose nature is more moist. For this very reason, then, fat melts easily under fire, but once melted does not easily congeal; suet, by contrast, does not melt easily, and if it is melted it congeals very quickly and appears much harder than fat. The pig, then, when well nourished, has a very great deal of fat on account of the natural moistness of its mixture, whereas oxen and goats and all other horned animals produce suet in greatest quantity on account of their dryness. You may, if you wish, call the whole oleaginous and oily substance in animals suet, as most physicians do; and you may also name this whole kind fat. You may not, however, if you wish not to speak falsely, say that the suet of goats is moister than that of pigs. For the latter is the moistest of nearly all the others, and for this reason in its action it is close to oil in power, though it is more softening than oil and more concoctive. And for this reason it is mixed into the poultices suited to inflammations. But for those who are bitten in the region of the rectum or the colon we inject goat's rather than pig's suet, not because it is more blunting of pungency (for, so far as its nature goes, pig's suet blunts more, and for this reason it is also mixed into drugs that treat ulcers, such as the one called parygron), but because goat's suet congeals easily on account of its thickness, whereas pig's flows away just like oil; for this reason we inject the former rather in dysenteric and tenesmic patients, when we wish to soothe their biting. Some things also blunt pungencies more on account of fineness of particles, when what is giving pain lies in the depth of the bodies that are being bitten. For the coarse-particled passes less, and the fine-particled more, through whole bodies, and mixes itself with all the biting ichors. In this respect, at any rate, goose suet blunts more effectively what bites in the depth of bodies. It is also hotter than pig's. Between them lies that of cocks; and that of male animals is always hotter and drier. Among these themselves, that of those whose testicles are excised is less hot and dry; for the castrated male always comes to resemble the females of the same kind. The general heading of the account, which must be remembered, is this: that the differences of the fat or suet in animals (or however you wish to name this whole kind of oleaginous and oily body in animals) always arise according to the mixture of the animals. Since, then, the pig falls short in heat and dryness of nearly all four-footed animals, for this reason it also has fat that is both less hot and more moist. Now the power of all fat is moistening and heating of human bodies; for this must be remembered throughout, having been stated at the beginning of this treatise; and the differences in each of them are matters of more and less. For the fat of the pig, relative to us, has moistening in abundance, but heating not in the same way as oil; rather, the fat (lipos) of pigs is on a level with the heat in us. That of bulls is much hotter and drier than that of pigs, we again remembering here too that the male is always drier and hotter than the female, and that the castrated male resembles the female, as indeed does everything young; and that among the young themselves the female is moister and less hot than the male. Thus calf's suet falls short of that of bulls in heat and dryness, and that of kids of that of goats, and that of goats falls short of that of he-goats, and again that of bulls in turn falls short of that of lions; for that is the most dispersing of nearly all those in quadrupeds. For it is also the hottest of them and the most fine-particled, so that if you mix it into the drugs suited to ulcers and inflammations, you will not only do no good but will even do harm, having made the drug more pungent than is fitting. But for chronic hardened swellings, contractures (ankylai) of sinews, and in short whatever is scirrhous, it is most suitable, in cases in which that of pigs appears most ineffective. That of bulls stands roughly equally distant from each, being as much hotter and drier than that of pigs as it falls short of that of lions. As being placed in the middle, then, it is reasonably mixed into each kind of drug, both those that treat scirrhous conditions and those that concoct inflamed parts, such as the one called tetrapharmakon, composed of wax, resin, pitch and suet. For into this, whether you put bull's or calf's or he-goat's or goat's or pig's, you will make a suppurative and concoctive drug. But pig's, when put in, suits children and women and, in a word, the soft-fleshed, while bull's is useful for diggers, reapers and all who have hard flesh, whether through their natural mixture or through the kind of life they lead. All suet, as it ages, becomes hotter and more fine-particled than it was, and so also more dispersing. This holds for nearly all things that age, at least those that do not rot first. For wine, honey, vinegar, cheese, butter and oesypum, and every oil, whether mastic oil or castor oil or radish oil or the very oil from olives, become thinner and more fine-particled as they age, and for this reason appear more pungent to those who taste them and more dispersing to those who use them, in all hardened and hard-to-resolve affections. I, then, have said that they appear more pungent to those who taste them. But some of those who corrupt the meanings of words call all such things not pungent but astringent, even down to pepper, as if it made no difference whether one says astringent or pungent. And if you ask them in turn about oak-galls, myrtles, medlars, pomegranate rinds, omphakion and sumach, they say that these too are astringent, although the perception we have from them is the most opposite to that which arises in us from pepper, pyrethron, mustard, euphorbium, onion, garlic and adarce. If, then, just as they apply the name of astringency in common to both of the affections arising in us in taste, so too they think that the affections themselves have a single nature, they must be considered anything rather than human beings, as alone possessing senses peculiar to themselves. But if they agree that unripe grape, sumach, oak-gall, balaustion and hypocistis draw together and constrict our substance, while pepper, mustard and pyrethron separate, bite and pass through it, then they improperly name the biting and heating things astringent, but they have not erred in their knowledge of the drugs themselves. If one must say by conjecture whether they err in the names or in the things, I for my part would say that these men have erred rather in the names, and especially those unaccustomed to the Greek tongue, such as Dioscorides of Anazarbus, who has said many things well among the matters recorded concerning medical material, but is ignorant of the meanings of Greek words. When this man, then, says that the suet of goats is more astringent than that of pigs, if by 'more astringent' he wishes to indicate 'more pungent', we shall accept the statement as true; but if he means what has the kind of quality and power that sumach and rhēon, hypocistis and balaustion have, we shall say that the statement is not true. Since they also say that the suet of vipers, when someone plucks out the hairs in the armpits by the roots and anoints the place, prevents these from growing again, I thought it better to test this too, and having done as they bade I found them to be lying, as also in the claim that it cures the beginnings of hypochyses. That the suet of bears suits alopecias they wrote truly, but we have more approved drugs than it for the affection. As for that of foxes, however, when they say that it treats earaches without distinguishing what sort of earaches they mean, as men who do not even know articulately what they are saying, one should not pay any attention to them at all. Others praise the suet of fishes, as being for hypochyses or whatever affection they wish to name. Others again write not simply of fishes, but of river fishes, and others rather of sea fishes, so that they may seem to know something more than the many.

    And since they say that the fat of vipers, too, when someone has plucked out from the roots the hairs in the armpits and smears it on, prevents these too from growing, it seemed to me better to test this as well, and having done as they bade I found them to be lying, just as also in their claim that it cures the beginnings of cataracts.
  • A · drug names: named in the Greek only (the name of the drug of entry 8.17.9)
    οὗτος οὖν, ὅταν λέγει στυπτικώτερον στέαρ εἶναι τὸ τῶν αἰγῶν τοῦ τῶν ὑῶν, εἰ μὲν οὖν τὸ δριμύτερον διὰ τοῦ στυπτικωτέρου βούλεται δηλῶσαι, ἐκδεξόμεθα τὸν λόγον ὡς ἀληθῆ· εἰ δὲ τὸ τοιαύτην ἔχον ποιότητά τε καὶ δύναμιν, οἷον ῥοῦς καὶ ῥῆον ὑπόκυστίς τε καὶ βαλαύστιον, οὐκ ἀληθῆ φήσομεν εἶναι τὸν λόγον.
    This man, then, when he says that the fat of goats is more astringent than that of pigs: if by more astringent he wishes to indicate more pungent, we shall accept the statement as true; but if he means that which has such a quality and power as sumac and rhubarb and hypokistis and balaustion, we shall say that the statement is not true.

    εἰ δ’ ὄμφακα μὲν καὶ ῥοῦν καὶ κηκῖδα βαλαύστιόν τε καὶ ὑποκυστίδα, συνάγειν τε καὶ σφίγγειν ἡμῶν ὁμολογοῦσι τὴν οὐσίαν, διακρίνειν τε καὶ δάκνειν καὶ διεξέρχεσθαι τὸ πέπερι καὶ νᾶπυ καὶ πύρεθρον, ἀκύρως μὲν ὀνομάζουσι στύφοντα τὰ δάκνοντα καὶ θερμαίνοντα, τῆς γνώσεως μέντοι τῶν φαρμάκων αὐτῶν οὐχ ἡμαρτήκασιν.
    But if they agree that unripe grape, sumach, oak-gall, balaustion and hypocistis draw together and constrict our substance, while pepper, mustard and pyrethron separate, bite and pass through it, then they improperly name the biting and heating things astringent, but they have not erred in their knowledge of the drugs themselves.
  • B · body parts: named in the English only (tongue)

    If one must say by conjecture whether they err in the names or in the things, I for my part would say that these men have erred rather in the names, and especially those unaccustomed to the Greek tongue, such as Dioscorides of Anazarbus, who has said many things well among the matters recorded concerning medical material, but is ignorant of the meanings of Greek words.

11.1.3 [γ΄. Περὶ μυελοῦ.] Μυελὸς μαλακτικῆς τῶν σκληρυνομένων τε καὶ σκιῤῥουμένων σωμάτων ἐστὶ δυνάμεως, εἴτε μύες εἴτε τένοντες εἴτε σύνδεσμοι τοῦτο πάσχοιεν εἴτε καὶ σπλάγχνα. καλλίστου δ’ ἐπειράθην ἀεὶ τοῦ τῶν ἐλάφων, ἐφεξῆς δ’ αὐτῶν τοῦ τῶν νέων βοῶν, οὓς δὴ καὶ μόσχους καλοῦσι, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο καὶ τὸν μυελὸν αὐτῶν ὀνομάζουσι μόσχειον. ὁ δὲ τῶν τράγων καὶ τῶν ταύρων δριμύτερος αὐτῶν ἐστι καὶ ξηραντικώτερος, ὥστ’ οὐ δύναται διαλύειν σκληρότητας σκιῤῥώδεις, εἴ τι μεμνήμεθα τῶν ἐν τῷ πέμπτῳ γράμματι περὶ τούτων εἰρημένων. ἐκ δ’ οὖν τοῦ μοσχείου γε καὶ ἐλαφείου μυελοῦ καὶ πεσσοὶ μαλακτικοὶ τῶν κατὰ τὰς μήτρας συντίθενται καὶ κατ’ αὐτῆς ἔξωθεν τῆς μήτρας ἐπιτίθεται φάρμακα διὰ μυελοῦ σκευαζόμενα, δύναμιν ἔχοντα μαλακτικήν. λαμβάνεται δ’ οὐ μόνος ὁ ἐκ τῶν ὀστῶν μυελὸς, ὅσπερ δὴ καὶ ὄντως ἐστὶ μυελὸς, ἀλλὰ καὶ ἐκ τῆς ῥάχεως, ὃν καὶ αὐτὸν ὀνομάζουσι μυελὸν νωτιαῖον, ὄντα σκληρότερόν τε καὶ αὐχμηρότερον τοῦ λοιποῦ. μαλακώτερος γάρ ἐστιν ἐκεῖνος καὶ λιπαρώτερος, καὶ διὰ τοῦτ’ ἐγὼ τὸν ἐκ τῆς ῥάχεως ἰδίᾳ καθ’ ἑαυτὸν ἄνευ τούτου κατατίθεμαι. φροντίζω δὲ τοῦ μήτε σαπῆναι μήτ’ εὐρῶτα σχεῖν ἑκάτερον τῶν μυελῶν. ἐν χειμῶνί τε οὖν πρῶτον αὐτοὺς λαμβάνω, καθάπερ καὶ τὸ στέαρ, εἶτ’ ἐν οἴκῳ ξηραίνων ὑπερώῳ τε καὶ ἀνίκμῳ μετὰ φύλλων δάφνης ξηρῶν ἀποτίθεμαι. τὰ γὰρ ὑγρὰ καὶ πρόσφατα φύλλα πολὺ τῆς ἑαυτῶν μεταδίδωσι ποιότητος, ὥστε δριμυτέρους γενέσθαι τοὺς μυελούς. εἰ δέ ποτε καὶ θερμοῦ καὶ νοτίου τοῦ περιέχοντος ὄντος ἀποθέσθαι βουληθείης μυελοὺς ἢ στέαρ, ἔστω σοι παρεσκευασμένος οἶκος μήτε θερμὸς, οἷοι μάλιστά εἰσιν οἱ πρὸς μεσημβρίαν ἐστραμμένοι, σήπεται γὰρ ἐν τοῖς τοιούτοις μήτε κατάγειος ὑγρὸς, εὐρωτιᾷ γὰρ ἐν τοῖς τοιούτοις, ἀλλ’ ὑπερῶός τε καὶ πρὸς ἄρκτον ἐστραμμένος, ἀναπεπταμένας ἔχων μικρὰς θυρίδας ὡς δέχεσθαι τοὺς ἀρκτικοὺς ἀνέμους δι’ ἡμέρας τε καὶ νυκτός.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: αὐχμηρότερον
11.1.3 [3. On marrow.] Marrow is of a softening power for bodies that are becoming hardened and scirrhous, whether it be muscles, tendons or ligaments that suffer this, or even viscera. I have always found that of deer the best, and next after it that of young cattle, which indeed they also call calves (moschoi), and for this reason they also name their marrow 'calf-marrow' (moscheion). That of he-goats and of bulls is more pungent than these and more drying, so that it cannot dissolve scirrhous hardnesses, if we remember at all what was said about these matters in the fifth book. From calf and deer marrow, then, softening pessaries are compounded for the affections of the womb, and medicines prepared with marrow, having a softening power, are applied externally over the womb itself. And not only the marrow from the bones is taken, which indeed is truly marrow, but also that from the spine, which they also call spinal marrow, being harder and drier (αὐχμηρότερον) than the other. For the latter is softer and oilier, and for this reason I store the marrow from the spine separately, by itself, without the other. I take care that neither of the two marrows should either rot or acquire mould. So I first take them in winter, just as the fat too, and then, drying them in a room that is upstairs and free of moisture, I store them away with dry bay leaves. For moist and fresh leaves impart much of their own quality, so that the marrows become more pungent. But if ever, when the surrounding air is hot and humid, you should wish to store away marrows or fat, let a room be prepared for you that is neither hot—such as those facing south especially are, for in such rooms they rot—nor underground and damp, for in such rooms they grow mouldy, but one that is upstairs and facing north, with small windows thrown open so as to receive the northerly winds by day and by night.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: αὐχμηρότερον, νοτίου, στέαρ
11.1.3 [3. On marrow.] Marrow is of a power softening of bodies that are hardened and becoming scirrhous, whether muscles or tendons or ligaments suffer this, or also viscera. I have always found by experience that the marrow of deer is the best, and next after it that of young cattle, whom they also call calves (moschoi), and for this reason they name their marrow too 'calf's marrow' (moscheios). That of he-goats and of bulls is more pungent than these and more drying, so that it cannot dissolve scirrhous hardnesses, if we remember anything of what was said about these matters in the fifth book. From calf's and deer's marrow, at any rate, softening pessaries are compounded for conditions in the uterus, and drugs prepared with marrow, having a softening power, are applied over the uterus itself from outside. Not only is the marrow from the bones taken, which is indeed marrow in the true sense, but also that from the spine, which they too name spinal marrow, being harder and more parched than the other. For the former is softer and more oily, and for this reason I store the marrow from the spine separately on its own, apart from the other. I take care that neither of the two marrows should rot or become mouldy. So first I take them in winter, just as I do the suet, then, drying them in an upper room free of moisture, I put them away with dry bay leaves. For moist and fresh leaves impart much of their own quality, so that the marrows become more pungent. And if ever you should wish to put away marrows or suet when the surrounding air is hot and humid, let a room be prepared for you that is neither hot, as are especially those facing south, for it rots in such rooms, nor underground and damp, for it grows mouldy in such rooms, but upper and facing north, having small windows open so as to receive the northern winds by day and by night.

11.1.4 [4. On the heads of maenides.] A certain man used the heads of salted maenides, by burning them, for fissures in the seat (anus); and this same man used them also for a uvula that had become chronically hardened (scirrhous). Their power, then, seems to be a drying one, not very pungent. For this latter property belongs to some of the burnt substances, whereas drying at least is common to them all.

11.1.7 [7. On the brain of a hare.] We have learned that the brain of a hare, both rubbed on and eaten, is beneficial in the case of children who are teething, the whole head having, of course, been boiled, as I am accustomed to do; its power, however, is not at all exceptional, but similar to honey and butter and the other things which are suitable for the teeth of growing children. Some have also written that this brain, when eaten, helps against fears.

11.1.8 [η΄. Περὶ κεράτων ἐλάφου καὶ αἰγῶν.] Κέρασιν ἐλάφου καὶ αἰγὸς κεκαυμένοις μάλιστα χρῶνται, καθάπερ καὶ ἡμεῖς ἐχρησάμεθα πολλάκις εἴς τε τὸ λαμπρύνειν τοὺς ὀδόντας καὶ προστέλλειν τὰ οὖλα τὰ πλαδαρά. τὸ δὲ τῆς ἐλάφου τινὲς τῶν γραψάντων τὰ τοιαῦτα μάλιστ’ ἐπαινοῦσιν, ὡς εἰ μετὰ τὸ καυθῆναι πλυθείη καὶ δυσεντερίαν καὶ πτύσιν ﻿ αἵματος, ἔτι τε τὰς καλουμένας κοιλιακὰς διαθέσεις ἐκθεραπεύειν, ἰκτερικοῖς τε διδόμενον ὠφέλιμον, ἐπὶ πάντων δὲ τούτων κελεύουσι διδόναι κοχλιάρια δύο, μεμίχασι δὲ αὐτό τινες, ὡς εἴρηται, κεκαυμένον τε καὶ πεπλυμένον καὶ τοῖς ξηραίνουσι κολλυρίοις τὰ ἐν ὀφθαλμοῖς ῥεύματα. ἔστι γὰρ δηλονότι πάντων τῶν τοιούτων ἡ δύναμις ξηραντικὴ, καθάπερ καὶ εἴρηται.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
11.1.8 [8. On the horns of deer and goats.] Horns of deer and of goat are used above all when burnt, just as we too have often used them both for whitening the teeth and for firming up flabby gums. Some of those who have written on such matters particularly commend that of the deer, claiming that, if after being burnt it is washed, it also thoroughly cures dysentery and spitting of blood, and further the so-called coeliac conditions, and that given to those suffering from jaundice it is beneficial; and in all these cases they prescribe giving two spoonfuls. Some have also mixed it, as has been said, burnt and washed, into the drying collyria for the fluxes in the eyes. For evidently the power of all such things is drying, as has also been said.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὡς εἰ μετὰ τὸ καυθῆναι πλυθείη ... ἐκθεραπεύειν (anacoluthic construction: conditional optative followed by an infinitive dependent on an implied verb of saying), μεμίχασι ... τοῖς ξηραίνουσι κολλυρίοις τὰ ἐν ὀφθαλμοῖς ῥεύματα (the accusative τὰ ῥεύματα is taken as the object of ξηραίνουσι)
11.1.8 [8. On the horns of deer and of goats.] People use the horns of deer and of goat chiefly when burnt, just as we too have often used them, both for brightening the teeth and for contracting gums that are flabby. Some of those who have written on such matters especially praise that of the deer, [saying] that, if after being burnt it is washed, it thoroughly cures dysentery and spitting of blood, and furthermore the so-called coeliac conditions, and that when given to those with jaundice it is beneficial; and in all these cases they direct that two spoonfuls be given. Some have also mixed it, burnt and washed as has been said, into the collyria that dry up the fluxes in the eyes. For the power of all such things is evidently drying, just as has also been said.
