11.1.15 [ιε΄. Περὶ καστορίου.] Ὄρχεις κάστορος ὀνομάζουσι καστόριον. ἔνδοξον δὲ καὶ πολύχρηστον φάρμακον, ὡς καὶ βιβλίον ὅλον Ἀρχιγένει γεγράφθαι περὶ καστορίου χρήσεως. ἐκεῖνος μὲν οὖν τὰς κατὰ μέρος αὐτοῦ δυνάμεις διῆλθεν, ἐγὼ δ’ ὥσπερ ἐπὶ τῶν ἄλλων φαρμάκων, οὕτω κᾀπὶ τούτου τὴν καθόλου δύναμιν ἐρῶ, πρὸς ἣν ἀποβλέπων τις εὑρίσκειν ἑαυτῷ δυνήσεται τὰς κατὰ μέρος. ὅτι μὲν δὴ θερμαίνει πρόδηλον. εἰ γὰρ ἐθελήσαις λεῖον ἀκριβῶς ἐργασάμενος αὐτὸ, δεύσας ἐλαίῳ, χρῖσαί τε καὶ ἀνατρῖψαι τι αὐτῷ μόριον, ἐναργῶς αἰσθήσῃ θερμαινόμενον αὐτό. τὰ δὲ θερμαίνοντα πάντα διαφοροῦντά τι τῆς πλησιαζούσης οὐσίας εὐθέως αὐτὴν καὶ ξηραίνει, πλὴν εἰ μή τι φύσει ὑγρὸν εἴη, καθάπερ ἔλαιόν τε καὶ ὕδωρ, ἐπίκτητόν τε ποιότητα θερμὴν, οὐ δύναμιν, προσλάβοι, καθάπερ αὐτὰ ταῦτα πυρὶ πλησιάσαντα καὶ ἡλίῳ θερινῷ. τοῦ τοίνυν καστορίου καὶ ἡ σύστασις οὖσα ξηρὰ προσειληφυῖά τε τὸ θερμαίνειν εἰκότως ξηραίνει. τοῦτο μὲν οὖν κοινὸν αὐτῷ καὶ ἄλλοις φαρμάκοις παμπόλλοις ἐστί· λεπτομερὲς δ’ ἱκανῶς ὑπάρχει καὶ κατὰ τοῦτο πλέον ἑτέρων δύναται, θερμαινόντων τε καὶ ξηραινόντων ὁμοίως αὐτῷ. τὰ γάρ τοι λεπτομερῆ φάρμακα δραστικώτερα τῶν παχυμερῶν ἐστι, κᾂν ἴσην ἔχῃ τὴν δύναμιν ἐκ τοῦ διεξέρχεσθαι καὶ κατὰ βάθους ἰέναι τῶν ὁμιλούντων σωμάτων, καὶ μάλισθ’ ὅταν ᾖ ταῦτα πυκνὰ, καθάπερ τὰ νευρώδη. φανερῶς γοῦν ὑπὸ τοῦ καστορίου τὰ τοιαῦτα μεγάλως ὠφελεῖται διὰ τὴν εἰρημένην αἰτίαν. ἀγνοοῦσι δὲ οἱ πλεῖστοι τῶν ἰατρῶν, ἐν τῇ τοῦ καστορίου χρήσει προσέχοντες τὸν νοῦν αὐτῷ μόνῳ τῶ τρέμειν ἢ σπᾶσθαί τι μόριον, ἢ ἀναίσθητον ἢ ἀκίνητον εἶναι, ἢ δυσαίσθητον ἢ δυσκίνητον, μὴ γινώσκοντες ἑπόμενα τοιαῦτα συμπτώματα διαθέσεσι σώματος ἀνομοίαις. ἀλλὰ σύ γε παρ’ Ἱπποκράτους μαθὼν ἐπὶ πληρώσει τε καὶ κενώσει γίγνεσθαι σπασμὸν, ἔνθα μὲν χρὴ κενῶσαι τὰ παρὰ φύσιν ἐν τοῖς νεύροις περιεχόμενα, καὶ πίνειν δίδου καὶ κατὰ τοῦ δέρματος ἔξωθεν ἐπιτίθει καστόριον. ἔνθα δὲ δι’ ὑπερβάλλουσαν ξηρότητα γίγνεται σπασμὸς, ἐναντιώτατον εἶναι γίγνωσκε τὸ φάρμακον τοῦτο. κατὰ δὲ τὸν αὐτὸν λόγον καὶ τοῖς τρέμουσι διὰ πλήθους ὠφελιμώτατόν ἐστιν, ἐναντιώτατον δὲ τοῖς διὰ κένωσιν εἰς τὸ τοιοῦτον πάθος ἐμπίπτουσιν. ἀνάλογον δὲ τοῖς εἰρημένοις πάθεσι καὶ τοῦ λύζειν ἐν τῷ στομάχῳ γιγνομένου τὴν διάθεσιν εὗρον καὶ διορισάμενος. εἰ μὲν ὑπὸ πλήθους τοῦτο πάσχοιεν οἱ κάμνοντες, ἐπὶ τὴν τοῦ καστορίου χρῆσιν ἧκε, διὰ ξηρότητα δὲ καὶ κένωσιν ἢ δι’ ὑγρῶν δριμέων δῆξιν εἰ συσταίη, φεῦγε τὸ φάρμακον. ὑπονοήσειε μὲν οὖν τις αὐτὸ τῇ τε ὀσμῇ καὶ τῇ γεύσει προσέχων ἀλλοτριωτάτην ἔχειν ἀνθρώπου σώματι τὴν οὐσίαν. ἐν μέντοι τῇ χρήσει φαίνεται μηδὲν ἐργαζόμενον ὧν τὰ τοιαῦτα ποιεῖ, τὰ μὲν στόμαχον κακοῦντα, τὰ δὲ γαστέρα, τὰ δὲ κεφαλὴν, τὰ δὲ ἄλλο τι. τουτὶ δὲ τὸ φάρμακον εἴθ’ ὑγρῷ σώματι προσφέροις δεομένῳ ξηρότητος εἴτε ψυχρῷ θερμότητος, εἴθ’ ὑγρῷ καὶ ψυχρῷ θερμότητος καὶ ξηρότητος χρῄζοντι, μεγάλην μὲν ὠφέλειαν ἐπιδείκνυται, βλάβην δ’ οὐδεμίαν οὐδενὶ ﻿ προστρίβεται μορίῳ, καὶ μάλισθ’ ὅταν ἀπύρετος ὁ ἄνθρωπος ᾖ, καὶ τῶν πυρεσσόντων δὲ μὴ πάνυ θερμῶς, ἀλλ’ ὡς ἂν εἴποι τις χλιαρὸν πυρετὸν, οἷος ἐν καταφοραῖς μάλιστα καὶ ληθάργοις γίγνεται. πολλοῖς μὲν πάνυ τὸ καστόριον ἐδώκαμεν, ἅμα μὲν πέπερι λευκῷ κοχλιαρίου πλῆθος ἑκατέρου διὰ μελικράτου ποτίζοντες, ἐβλάβη δ’ οὐδεὶς οὐδὲν, ἀλλὰ καταμηνίων ἐπεχομένων προκενώσας ἀπὸ σφυροῦ μετρίως, εἶτα διδοὺς καστόριον μετὰ γλήχωνος ἢ καλαμίνθης, ἐπειράθην ἀεὶ τοῦ φαρμάκου κινοῦντος κάθαρσιν, ἄνευ τοῦ βλαβῆναί τι τὴν ἄνθρωπον. ἐκβάλλει δὲ καὶ χόρια κατεσχημένα. ταῦτα μὲν οὖν ἅπαντα διὰ μελικράτου πινόμενον ἐργάζεται. τοὺς δὲ ἐμπνευματουμένους τὴν γαστέρα δυσιάτως ἢ στροφουμένους ἢ λύζοντας, ἐπὶ ψυχροῖς καὶ γλίσχροις χυμοῖς ἢ παχεῖ καὶ φυσώδει πνεύματι, δι’ ὀξυκράτου πινόμενον ὀνίνησιν· ὅσα δὲ εἴσω τοῦ σώματος λαμβανόμενον ὠφελεῖ, ταῦτα καὶ κατὰ τοῦ δέρματος ἐπιτιθέμενον ἅμα σικυωνίῳ ἢ παλαιῷ ἐλαίῳ. τὰ δὲ πλείονος θερμότητος δεόμενα καὶ ἀνατρίβειν προσήκει δι’ ἑαυτοῦ. ὠφελεῖ δὲ καὶ εἴ τις ἐπ’ ἄνθραξιν αὐτὸ θυμιῶν ἀρύῃ διὰ τῆς εἰσπνοῆς, καὶ μάλιστα τὰ κατὰ τὸν πνεύμονα καὶ ἐγκέφαλον ὑγρὰ καὶ ψυχρὰ πάθη. τά γε μὴν ληθαργικὰ καὶ καταφορικὰ πάντα πάθη μετὰ πυρετῶν ἄμεινον οὐ τῶν εἰρημένων ἐλαίων τινὶ δεύοντας θεραπεύειν, ἀλλὰ διὰ ῥοδίνου μᾶλλον ἐπιτιθέναι τῇ κεφαλῇ καὶ τῷ αὐχένι.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἀρύῃ, σικυωνίῳ, δι’ ἑαυτοῦ
11.1.15 [15. On castoreum.] They call the testicles of the beaver castoreum. It is a renowned and much-used drug, so much so that a whole book was written by Archigenes on the use of castoreum. He, then, went through its particular powers; but I, just as with the other drugs, so also with this one shall state its general power, by looking to which one will be able to discover the particular ones for oneself. That it heats is evident. For if you should wish, having worked it perfectly smooth and moistened it with oil, to anoint and rub some part with it, you will clearly perceive that part being heated. Now all heating things, dispersing something of the adjacent substance, immediately also dry it, unless the thing be moist by nature, like oil and water, and take on an acquired hot quality, not a power, just as these very things do when brought near fire and the summer sun. Accordingly castoreum, its consistency being dry and having in addition the heating [property], reasonably dries. This, then, is common to it and to very many other drugs; but it is sufficiently fine-particled, and in this respect it is more powerful than others which heat and dry similarly to it. For fine-particled drugs are more active than coarse-particled ones, even if they have equal power, from their passing through and going into the depth of the bodies with which they come into contact, and especially when these are dense, like the sinewy parts. Clearly, at any rate, such parts are greatly benefited by castoreum for the reason stated. But most physicians are ignorant, in the use of castoreum attending in their minds only to the fact that some part trembles or is convulsed, or is without sensation or without motion, or has impaired sensation or impaired motion, not knowing that such symptoms follow upon dissimilar conditions of the body. But you, having learned from Hippocrates that spasm occurs upon repletion and upon evacuation, where it is necessary to evacuate what is contained contrary to nature in the nerves, both give castoreum to drink and apply it externally upon the skin; but where spasm occurs through excessive dryness, know that this drug is most contrary. By the same reasoning it is also most beneficial to those who tremble because of plethora, but most contrary to those who fall into such an affection through evacuation. Analogously to the affections mentioned I have found also the condition of the hiccuping that arises in the stomach, having made the distinction: if the patients suffer this from plethora, come to the use of castoreum; but if it should arise through dryness and evacuation or through the biting of pungent humours, avoid the drug. One might indeed suspect, attending to its smell and taste, that it has a substance most alien to the human body. In use, however, it appears to do none of the things which such substances do, some harming the stomach, some the belly, some the head, some something else. But this drug, whether you administer it to a moist body needing dryness, or to a cold one [needing] heat, or to a moist and cold one requiring heat and dryness, displays great benefit and inflicts no harm upon any part, and especially when the person is without fever, and, among those with fever, when the fever is not very hot but what one might call a lukewarm fever, such as arises especially in comatose states (kataphorai) and lethargies. To very many we have given castoreum, giving to drink together with white pepper a spoonful of each in honey-water (melikraton), and no one was harmed in any way; rather, when the menses were retained, having first evacuated moderately from the ankle, then giving castoreum with pennyroyal (glechon) or kalaminthe (calamint), I have always found the drug moving a purgation, without the woman being harmed in anything. It also expels retained afterbirths (choria). All these things, then, it does when drunk in honey-water (melikraton). Those whose belly is intractably filled with wind, or who suffer colic or hiccup, in consequence of cold and viscous humours or of thick and flatulent pneuma, it helps when drunk in oxykraton (vinegar-and-water); and whatever it benefits when taken inside the body, these it benefits also when applied upon the skin together with Sicyonian oil or old oil. For the conditions needing more heat it is appropriate also to rub [the part] with it by itself. It helps too if someone, burning it on coals as a fumigation, draws it in through inhalation, and especially the moist and cold affections of the lung and the brain. The lethargic and comatose affections, however, all those with fevers, it is better to treat not by moistening it with any of the oils mentioned, but rather to apply it in rose oil to the head and the neck.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: εὗρον καὶ διορισάμενος (syntax defective; perhaps read εὑρὼν καὶ διορισάμενος), τῶ τρέμειν (read τῷ), ἀρύῃ, δυσιάτως, καταφοραῖς
11.1.15 [15. On castoreum.] The testicles of the beaver they call castoreum. It is a celebrated drug with many uses, so much so that Archigenes wrote a whole book on the use of castoreum. He went through its particular powers one by one. I, as with the other drugs, so with this one too, shall state its general power; by looking to this, one will be able to discover the particular ones for oneself. That it heats is quite evident. For if you grind it exactly smooth, moisten it with oil, and then anoint and rub some part with it, you will clearly perceive the part being heated. All things that heat, by dispersing some of the substance that lies near them, at once also dry it. The exception is something moist by nature, such as oil and water, which takes on an acquired hot quality and not a power, as these very things do when brought near fire or the summer sun. Castoreum, then, since its consistency is dry and it has also acquired the capacity to heat, reasonably dries. This it has in common with very many other drugs. But it is sufficiently fine-particled, and in this respect it is more powerful than other drugs that heat and dry to the same degree as it does. For fine-particled drugs are more active than coarse-particled ones, even if they have an equal power, because they pass through and go into the depth of the bodies they come into contact with, especially when these are dense, as the sinewy ones are. At any rate, such parts are clearly greatly benefited by castoreum for the stated cause. Most physicians, however, are ignorant of this. In using castoreum they attend only to the fact that some part trembles or is convulsed, or is insensible or motionless, or has impaired sensation or impaired motion. They do not know that such symptoms follow unlike conditions of the body. But you have learnt from Hippocrates that convulsion arises both from repletion and from evacuation. Where it is necessary to evacuate what is contained in the nerves contrary to nature, give castoreum to drink and also apply it externally upon the skin. But where convulsion arises through excessive dryness, know that this drug is most contrary. By the same reasoning it is most beneficial to those who tremble through plethos, but most contrary to those who fall into such an affection through evacuation. Analogously to the affections mentioned, I found and distinguished the condition of the hiccup that arises in the stomach. If the patients suffer this from plethos, proceed to the use of castoreum; but if it arises through dryness and evacuation, or through the biting of pungent fluids, avoid the drug. Now someone attending to its smell and taste might suspect that it has a substance most alien to the human body. In use, however, it appears to do none of the things that such substances do, some of them harming the stomach, others the belly, others the head, others some other part. This drug, by contrast, displays great benefit and inflicts no harm whatever on any part. This holds whether you apply it to a moist body needing dryness, or to a cold one needing heat, or to a moist and cold one requiring heat and dryness. It holds especially when the person is without fever. It holds also for those who are feverish, but not very hotly, having rather what one might call a lukewarm fever, such as occurs above all in kataphorai and lethargies. We have given castoreum to very many people, together with white pepper, a spoonful's quantity of each, given to drink in honey-water, and no one was harmed in any way. When the menses were retained, I first evacuated moderately from the ankle, then gave castoreum with pennyroyal or kalaminthē. I always found that the drug set purgation in motion, without the woman being harmed at all. It also expels retained afterbirths. All these things, then, it accomplishes when drunk in honey-water. Some people are intractably inflated in the belly, or suffer gripes, or hiccup, from cold and viscid humours or from thick and flatulent pneuma. These it benefits when drunk in oxycrate. Whatever it benefits when taken into the body, it also benefits when applied upon the skin together with Sicyonian or old oil. Conditions needing more heat should also be rubbed with it on its own. It also benefits if one burns it as a fumigation on coals and draws it in by inhalation, especially for the moist and cold affections of the lung and brain. All lethargic and kataphoric affections accompanied by fevers, however, are better treated not by moistening it with one of the oils mentioned, but rather by applying it with rose oil to the head and the neck.

11.1.16 [ιστ΄. Περὶ νεφρῶν σκίγκων.] Τὰ περὶ τοὺς νεφροὺς τῶν σκίγκων ὡς ἐντατικὰ αἰδοίων πίνεται καὶ ἀντιπεπονθέναι γε αὐτοῖς δοκεῖ, μάλιστα θρίδακος σπέρμα μεθ’ ὕδατος πινόμενον. ἔνιοι δὲ καὶ τὸ τῶν φακῶν ἀφέψημα μετὰ μέλιτος πινόμενον παύειν φασὶ τὰς ἐντάσεις.

11.1.16 [16. On the kidneys of skinks.] The parts around the kidneys of skinks are drunk as being productive of tension in the genital member, and what seems to act antagonistically to them is above all lettuce seed drunk with water. Some people also say that a decoction of lentils drunk with honey stops the tensions.

11.1.16 [16. On the kidneys of skinks.] The parts around the kidneys of skinks are drunk as tensive of the genitals, and lettuce seed drunk with water is thought to be especially counteractive to them. Some people also say that the decoction of lentils, drunk with honey, stops the tensions.

11.1.18 [18. On burnt bones.] Burnt bones are of a sufficiently dispersing and drying power, and some people say that those of human beings are most of all capable of this. The astragalos of a pig, at any rate, when burnt and then drunk, they said cures flatulent distensions and colics. And I have known some of the men of our own time who, giving burnt human bones to drink, without those drinking knowing what they were drinking, so that they should not feel disgust, completely cured many of epilepsy and arthritis.

And I have known some people of our time who gave burnt bones to drink, without those drinking knowing what they were drinking, so that they would not be disgusted, and who had thoroughly cured many people of epilepsy and arthritis.

11.1.23 [κγ΄. Περὶ δερμάτων παλαιῶν.] Δέρματα παλαιὰ τὰ ἀπὸ τῶν καττυμάτων καυθέντα πρὸς τὰ ἐκ τῶν ὑποδημάτων ἐκθλίμματα γεγράφασιν ἁρμόττειν, ὡς δῆθεν ἀντιπαθείᾳ τινὶ τοῦτο ποιοῦντα. φλεγμαίνοντα μὲν οὖν οὐκ ὠφελεῖται, παυσαμένης δὲ τῆς φλεγμονῆς εἰκότως ὠφελεῖται ξηραινόμενα· ξηραντικὴ γὰρ ἡ τοιαύτη τέφρα. καὶ ἡμεῖς ἐχρησάμεθά ποτε κατὰ ἀγρὸν αὐτῇ πρός τι τοιοῦτον ἐκ παρατρίμματος ἕλκος. θεραπεύει δ’ εἰκότως ἡ τοιαύτη τέφρα καὶ τὰ πυρίκαυτα τῶν ἑλκῶν καὶ τὰ καλούμενα παρατρίμματα κατὰ τοὺς μηροὺς γινόμενα.

11.1.23 [23. On old hides.] Old hides from shoe-soles, when burnt, they have written to be suitable for the pressure-sores caused by shoes, as though, forsooth, they did this by some antipathy. Now while these are inflamed they are not helped, but once the inflammation has ceased they are reasonably helped by being dried; for such ash is drying. And we ourselves once used it in the country for such an ulcer arising from chafing. And such ash reasonably treats also the burns among ulcers and the so-called chafings that occur on the thighs.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: καττυμάτων, ἐκθλίμματα, παρατρίμματος, παρατρίμματα
11.1.23 [23. On old leathers.] Old leathers, those from shoe-soles, when burnt, are recorded as suitable for the chafings (ekthlimmata) caused by shoes, as though, forsooth, they did this by some antipathy. While the chafings are inflamed, then, they are not benefited; but once the inflammation has ceased, they are with good reason benefited by being dried, for such ash is drying. I myself also once used it in the country for an ulcer of this kind arising from a chafe (paratrimma). Such ash also, with good reason, heals the burns among ulcers and the so-called chafes (paratrimmata) that occur on the thighs.

11.1.24 [24. On ostreia (shellfish).] Some people call 'ostreia' all the testaceous animals (ostrakoderma), as they were named by Aristotle. But what is called by most people 'ostreon', pronounced without the iota in the second syllable, they posit as one particular species of the ostreia, including in the whole genus both the whelks (kērykes) and the purple-shells (porphyrai) and the clams (chēmai) and the pinnas and all the similar creatures, about which I shall speak in what follows.

11.1.24 [24. On ostreia.] Some people call ostreia all the creatures that were named ostracoderms ('shell-skinned') by Aristotle. The one that most people call ostreon, pronounced without the iota in the second syllable, they set down as one particular species of the ostreia, and in the genus as a whole they include trumpet-shells (kērykes), purple-shells (porphyrai), clams (chēmai) and pinnas (pinai), and all the similar creatures, which I shall discuss next.

Simp.11.1.25Simp. 11.1.25, ch. 11.1 difficulty mid (2.94/100; thin 1, absent 3) (a) pass (b) pass 6/8 (c) pass 16/16 unit: pass
Greek (Kühn)
11.1.25 [κε΄. Περὶ κηρύκων καὶ πορφυρῶν.] Κηρύκων καὶ πορφυρῶν τὸ ὄστρακον, ἐπειδὴ σκληρότατόν ἐστιν, οὐδέποτ’ αὐτῷ χωρὶς καύσεως κέχρημαι. καυθὲν μέντοι ξηραντικῆς ἐστι δυνάμεως, ἀκριβῶς δ’ αὐτὸ χρὴ λειοῦν, ὡς χνοῶδες γίνεσθαι, κόψαντα δηλονότι τι καὶ σήσαντα πρότερον. ἔστω δέ σοι τοῦτο κοινὸν ἐπὶ πάντων τῶν λιθώδη καὶ ὀστρακώδη τὴν οὐσίαν ἐχόντων. εἰ μὴ γὰρ ἀκριβῶς λειωθείη, παραπλήσια ταῖς ψάμμοις ἐστί. καὶ αὐτὸ δὲ καθ’ αὑτὸ λειωθέντων αὐτῶν τὸ γινόμενον ἄλευρον ἕλκεσι κακοήθεσιν ἁρμόττει. κοινὸν δὲ καὶ τοῦτ’ αὐτὸ πάντων ἐστὶ τῶν ξηραινόντων ἰσχυρῶς, ἄνευ τοῦ δάκνειν ἐπιφανῶς· ἐρεθίζοντα γὰρ ἐκεῖνα ῥευμάτων αἴτια γίνεται. καὶ ἄλλο δέ τι κοινὸν ὑπάρχει τοῖς τοιούτοις ἅπασι, μετ’ ὀξυκράτου γὰρ ἢ ὄξους ἢ οἰνομέλιτος ἢ ὀξυμέλιτος ἐπιτήδεια τοῖς σηπεδονώδεσιν ἕλκεσι γίγνεται. διὰ τοῦτο καὶ οἱ λίθοι πάντες εἰς τοιαύτην ἀγόμενοι χρείαν οἱ μὲν μᾶλλον, οἱ δ’ ἧττον, ἅπαντες δ’ αὖ ὠφελοῦσι.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τι (in κόψαντα δηλονότι τι καὶ σήσαντα), αὖ (in ἅπαντες δ’ αὖ ὠφελοῦσι)
11.1.25 [25. On whelks (kerykes) and purple-shells (porphyrai).] The shell of whelks and purple-shells, since it is extremely hard, I have never used without burning. When burnt, however, it is of a drying power, and it must be ground finely, so as to become dust-like, having of course first pounded it somewhat and sifted it. Let this be a common rule for you in the case of all things that have a stony and shell-like substance. For if it is not finely ground, they are like sands. And the meal that results from grinding them is, in itself and on its own, suitable for malignant ulcers. This very point too is common to all things that dry strongly without biting noticeably; for those that bite, by irritating, become causes of fluxes. And there is yet another thing common to all such things: with oxykraton (vinegar-and-water) or vinegar or honeyed wine or oxymel they become suitable for putrefactive ulcers. For this reason all stones too, when brought to such a use, some more and some less, but all in turn, are of benefit.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: χνοῶδες, καὶ αὐτὸ δὲ καθ’ αὑτὸ λειωθέντων αὐτῶν τὸ γινόμενον ἄλευρον, ἄλευρον, δ’ αὖ
11.1.25 [25. On trumpet-shells and purple-shells.] The shell of trumpet-shells (kērykes) and purple-shells (porphyrai), since it is extremely hard, I have never used without burning. Burnt, however, it is of a drying power; but it must be ground precisely, so that it becomes downy-fine, having of course first pounded and sifted it. Let this be a rule common to all things that have a stony or shell-like substance. For if it is not ground precisely, it is much like grains of sand. And the meal produced when they are ground, used by itself, is suited to malignant ulcers. This very property is also common to all things that dry strongly without conspicuously biting; for those that bite, by irritating, become causes of fluxes. And there is something else common to all such things: with oxycrate or vinegar or honeyed wine [oinomeli] or oxymel they become serviceable for putrefactive ulcers. For this reason all the stones too, when brought to such a use, are beneficial, some more, some less, but all of them nonetheless.

11.1.26 [κστ΄. Περὶ ὀστρέων.] Ὀστρέων τὸ ὄστρακον καυθὲν ὁμοίας ἐστὶ δυνάμεως τῷ τῶν κηρύκων ὀστράκῳ, καὶ μᾶλλον ἔτι λεπτομερεστέρας, ἀσφαλέστερον δ’ εἰπεῖν, ἧττον παχυμεροῦς. ἅπαντα γὰρ γεώδη καὶ σκληρὰν τὴν τοῦ σώματος ἔχοντα σύστασίν τε καὶ πῆξιν ἱκανῶς ἐστι παχυμερῆ, διὸ καὶ λειοῦν ἐπιμελῶς αὐτὰ προσήκει. ὅταν οὖν ποτ’ εἴπωμεν ἕτερον ἑτέρου τούτων εἶναι λεπτομερέστερον, οὕτως ἀκούειν χρὴ τῆς λέξεως, ὡς εἰ καὶ τὸν Ὀδυσσέα φαίημεν εἶναι τοῦ Θερσίτου μείζονα, μικρῶν ἀμφοτέρων ὄντων, ἢ τὸν Αἴαντα τοῦ Ἀχιλλέως μικρότερον, ἀμφοῖν καὶ τούτων μεγάλων ὄντων. ἔχει δ’, ὡς εἴρηται καὶ πρόσθεν, ἅπαντα τὰ τοιαῦτα τῆς συνακτικῆς τι δυνάμεως ἐν ἑαυτοῖς, δι’ ἣν καὶ πιληθείσης αὐτῶν τῆς οὐσίας σκληρὰ καὶ γεώδη γέγονεν, ὃ διὰ τῆς καύσεως ἀποβαλόντα τὴν ἐναντίαν ἐπικτᾶται δύναμιν, ἣν διαφορητικὴν ὀνομάζομεν. εἰ δὲ πλύναις αὐτὰ καὶ καύσαις, τὸ μὲν οἷον ἐμπύρευμα πᾶν ἐναποτίθεται τῷ ὕδατι, καὶ τοῦτό γ’ ἐργάζεται θερμαῖνον λεπτομερῶς, ὡς καὶ σήπειν ἐνίοτε. τὸ δὲ ὑπόλοιπον ἄδηκτον γίγνεται γεῶδες, ὃ μάλιστά ἐστιν ἐπὶ τῶν ὑγρῶν ἑλκῶν χρήσιμον, εἰς πλήρωσίν τε καὶ συνούλωσιν. τῷ δ’ οὖν ὀστράκῳ τῶν ὀνομαζομένων ἰδίως ὀστρέων κεκαυμένῳ χρῶμαι πρὸς τὰς ἐκ ῥευμάτων χρονίας καὶ δυσσαρκώτους κοιλότητας, ὅσαι συραγγώδεις εἰσὶ καὶ βαθεῖαι, περιτεθεὶς ἔξωθεν αὐτὸ μετὰ παλαιοῦ στέατος ὑείου. καλεῖται δ’ ὑπὸ τῶν πολλῶν ἀξούγγιον τοῦτο. καὶ εἴς γε τὸν κόλπον αὐτὸν ἐμβάλλω τι τῶν τὰ τοιαῦτα σαρκούντων, οἷόν ἐστι καὶ τὸ κεκαυμένον διφρυγὲς, ὃ καλοῦσι σπεκάριον. ὁμοίας δὲ κατὰ γένος ἐστὶ δυνάμεως καὶ τὰ τῶν ἄλλων ὀστρακοδέρμων ὄστρακα καυθέντα, μάλιστα δ’, ὡς ἔφην, τὸ τῶν ὀστρέων, εἶτα τῶν κηρύκων τε καὶ πορφυρῶν. μίγνυταί τε οὖν ἐμπλαστικαῖς δυνάμεσι διαφορητικαῖς ἡ τοιαύτη τέφρα καὶ μετὰ στέατος ἅπαντος διαφορεῖ. τῷ δ’ εἶναι διαφορητικώτερον τὸ παλαιὸν ἐκείνῳ μᾶλλον μίγνυται. δριμύτερον δὲ τοῦ προσή κοντος γίνεται τὸ φάρμακον, εἰ τῶν δριμέων τι μίξειας αὐτῷ στεάτων, ὑπὲρ ὧν ἔμπροσθεν διωρισάμην. ἀλλὰ καὶ λαμπρύνει τοὺς ὀδόντας ἡ τῶν τοιούτων ἁπάντων τέφρα, οὐ μόνῳ τῷ ῥυπτικῷ τῆς δυνάμεως, ἀλλὰ καὶ τῷ τῆς οὐσίας τραχεῖ, καθάπερ ἡ κίσσηρίς τε καὶ τὸ τῶν κλιβάνων ὄστρακον. ἐν μὲν οὖν τῇ τοιαύτῃ χρήσει πάνυ σφόδρα λειοῦν οὐκ ἀναγκαῖόν ἐστι τὰ οὕτω καυθέντα, τοῖς δὲ κακοήθεσιν ἕλκεσιν ἐπὶ πάντων ἀκριβῶς προλειώσεις. καὶ τὰ ὑπερσαρκοῦντα δὲ μετρίως καθαιρεῖ καὶ προστέλλει. μετά γε μὴν ἁλῶν πάντα τὰ τοιαῦτα καυθέντα ποιεῖ μὲν καὶ τὸ τῶν ὀδόντων σμῆγμα δραστικώτερον, ὡς τὰ πλαδαρὰ τῶν οὔλων ξηραίνειν, ἀλλὰ καὶ τὰ σηπεδονώδη τῶν ἑλκῶν ὠφελεῖ.

11.1.26 [26. On oysters.] The shell of oysters, when burnt, is of a power similar to the shell of the trumpet-shells, and still more fine-particled, or, to put it more safely, less coarse-particled. For all things whose body has an earthy and hard consistency and solidification are considerably coarse-particled, and for this reason it is also fitting to pulverize them carefully. So whenever we say that one of these is more fine-particled than another, one must understand the expression in this way: as if we were to say that Odysseus is bigger than Thersites, both of them being small, or that Ajax is smaller than Achilles, both of these too being big. All such things have in themselves, as has also been said before, something of the contracting power, through which, their substance having been compacted, they have become hard and earthy; and when they cast this off through burning they acquire in addition the opposite power, which we call dispersing. If you wash them and burn them, the whole of what is, as it were, the fiery residue (ἐμπύρευμα) is deposited in the water, and this it accomplishes by heating in a fine-particled way, so as sometimes even to putrefy. The remainder becomes earthy and non-biting, and this is especially useful for moist ulcers, for filling and for cicatrization. So I use the burnt shell of what are specifically called oysters for the chronic cavities arising from fluxes that are hard to fill with flesh, those that are fistulous and deep, applying it around on the outside together with old pig's fat. This is called by most people axoungion. And into the sinus itself I insert one of the things that make flesh grow in such cases, such as burnt diphryges, which they call spekarion. Of similar power in kind are also the burnt shells of the other shell-skinned animals, but most of all, as I said, that of oysters, then that of trumpet-shells and purple-shells. Such ash, then, is mixed into clogging, dispersing powers, and together with every kind of fat it disperses. But since old fat is more dispersing, it is mixed rather with that. The drug becomes more pungent than is fitting if you mix with it one of the pungent fats, about which I drew distinctions earlier. But the ash of all such things also brightens the teeth, not only by the cleansing element of its power but also by the roughness of its substance, just like pumice and the potsherd of ovens. In such a use, then, it is not necessary to pulverize very finely the things so burnt, but for malignant ulcers you will in all cases pulverize them finely beforehand. And it also moderately reduces and represses excess growths of flesh. With salt, indeed, all such things when burnt make the dentifrice more effective, so as to dry flabby gums, but they also help putrid ulcers.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: πλύναις αὐτὰ καὶ καύσαις, ἐμπύρευμα, σπεκάριον, τὸ τῶν κλιβάνων ὄστρακον, προστέλλει, σμῆγμα
11.1.26 [26. On oysters.] The shell of oysters, when burnt, is of a power similar to that of the shell of whelks, and still more fine-particled, or, to put it more safely, less coarse-particled. For all things which have an earthy and hard consistency and solidity of body are quite coarse-particled, which is why it is proper to grind them carefully. So whenever we say that one of these is more fine-particled than another, the expression must be understood in the same way as if we were to say that Odysseus is bigger than Thersites, though both are small, or that Ajax is smaller than Achilles, though both of these are big. As has also been said before, all such things have in themselves something of the contracting power, on account of which, their substance having been compacted, they have also become hard and earthy; and having cast this off through burning, they acquire the opposite power, which we call dispersing. If you wash them and burn them, all of what is, as it were, the fiery residue is deposited in the water, and this acts by heating in a fine-particled way, so as at times even to cause putrefaction. The remainder becomes earthy and non-biting, which is especially useful on moist ulcers, for filling them up and bringing them to close with a scar. I use the burnt shell of those that are properly called oysters for the chronic cavities that arise from fluxes and are hard to fill with flesh, those that are fistulous and deep, applying it on the outside with old pig's fat; this is called axungia by most people. And into the sinus itself I put one of the things that fill such places with flesh, such as burnt diphryges, which they call spekarion. The shells of the other testaceans too, when burnt, are of a power similar in kind, but especially, as I said, that of oysters, and next that of whelks and purple-shells. Such ash, then, is mixed into clogging dispersing drugs, and with any fat it disperses. Because old fat is more dispersing, it is mixed rather with that. The drug becomes more pungent than is proper if you mix with it one of the pungent fats, about which I made distinctions earlier. But the ash of all such things also brightens the teeth, not only by the cleansing quality of its power but also by the roughness of its substance, just as pumice and oven-potsherd do. Now in this kind of use it is not necessary to grind very thoroughly the things burnt in this way, but for malignant ulcers you will in all cases grind them precisely beforehand. They also moderately reduce and repress excess flesh. Moreover, all such things burnt with salt make the tooth-cleansing powder more effective, so as to dry the flabby parts of the gums, and they also benefit putrefying ulcers.

11.1.27 [κζ΄. Περὶ σηπίας.] Ἱκανῶς τὸ σηπίας ὄστρακον χαῦνόν ἐστιν, οὐχ ὥσπερ τὰ τῶν ὀστρείων λιθώδη. τὸ μὲν οὖν ῥύπτειν, ὥσπερ καὶ τἄλλα τῶν ὀστρακοδέρμων ὄστρακα, κοινὸν αὐτῷ πρὸς ἐκεῖνα, καθάπερ γε καὶ τὸ ξηραίνειν. τὸ δὲ τῆς οὐσίας λεπτομερὲς ἐξαίρετον αὐτῷ παρὰ τἄλλα ὑπάρχει, διὸ καὶ καυθέντι χρώμεθα πρὸς ἀλφοὺς ἔφηλίν τε καὶ φακοὺς καὶ ψώρας. ἀλλὰ καὶ τοῖς ὀρυκτοῖς ἁλσὶ μιχθὲν ἀποτήκει τὰ κατ’ ὀφθαλμοὺς γινόμενα πτερύγια. καὶ πρὶν καυθῆναι δὲ ὀπτώμενόν τε καὶ λειούμενον ὀδόντας τε λαμπρύνει καὶ ξηραίνει τὰ ἕλκη. τῷ συμμέτρῳ αὐτοῦ τῆς τραχύτητος καὶ πρὸς τὰς ἐν ὀφθαλμοῖς μεγάλας τραχύτητας, ἃς καλοῦσιν συκώσεις, εἰώθαμεν χρῆσθαι, διαγλύφοντες ἐξ αὐτοῦ παραπλήσιόν τι κολλυρίῳ κατὰ τὸ σχῆμα καὶ ἀνατρίβοντες αὐτὰς ὡς αἱμάξαι. τούτου γὰρ γενομένου καὶ τὰ καθαιρετικὰ τῶν κολλυρίων ἄμεινον ἐπ’ αὐτῶν ἐνεργεῖ.

11.1.27 [27. On the cuttlefish.] The shell of the cuttlefish is quite loose-textured, not stony like those of oysters. Now cleansing, as with the other shells of the shell-skinned animals, is common to it along with them, just as drying is too. But the fine-particled quality of its substance belongs to it as something exceptional compared with the others, which is why we also use it, when burnt, for alphoi, ephelis, lentigines and scabies. Moreover, mixed with mined salt it melts away the pterygia that form in the eyes. And even before being burnt, when roasted and finely ground, it both brightens the teeth and dries wounds. On account of the moderate degree of its roughness we are also accustomed to use it for the great roughnesses in the eyes which they call sycoses, carving out of it something resembling a collyrium in shape and rubbing them with it so as to draw blood. For when this has been done, the reducing collyria too act better upon them.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: ὀπτώμενόν, χαῦνόν, ὀρυκτοῖς
11.1.27 [27. On the cuttlefish.] The shell of the cuttlefish is quite porous, not stony like those of oysters. Cleansing, then, it has in common with them, as do the other shells of the testaceans, just as it also has drying. But the fine-particled nature of its substance belongs to it as a special property beyond the others; for this reason we also use it burnt for alphoi, ephelis, phakoi (lentil-spots) and psorai. Moreover, mixed with mined salt, it melts away the pterygia that arise on the eyes. And even before it is burnt, when roasted and ground smooth, it brightens the teeth and dries ulcers. We are also accustomed to use the moderate degree of its roughness against the great roughnesses in the eyes which they call sykōseis: we carve out of it something resembling a collyrium in shape and rub them with it so as to draw blood. For when this has been done, the reducing collyria also act better upon them.

    On the cuttlefish.] The shell of the cuttlefish is quite loose-textured, not stony like those of oysters.

Rendering A (fable-5-1)
11.1.29 [29. On oesypum-bearing wool.] Wool that is still dirty, and for this reason called by some "oesypum-bearing", usefully receives the affusions applied to parts that have been bruised or struck in any way whatever, contributing to the benefit derived from them by means of the oesypum. Wool that has been washed and no longer contains the oesypum becomes a kind of material suitable for receiving the moistures that are poured on. When burnt, it has a power that is pungent and hot together with fineness of particles, so that it melts away even the flabby flesh upon ulcers very quickly. It is also put into a drying drug; and burn it just as also most of the others, filling a new earthen pot and then covering it with a lid that has several holes.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: οἰσυπηρῶν, οἰσυπηρὰ, ἐπιβροχὰς, λεπτομερείᾳ
11.1.29 [29. On oesypous wool.] Wool that is still dirty, and for this reason called by some 'oesypous', usefully takes up the embrocations applied to parts that have been bruised or struck in any way whatever, and it contributes to the benefit derived from them on account of its oesypum. Wool that has been washed and no longer has its oesypum becomes a suitable material for taking up the liquids poured over it. When burnt, it has a power that is pungent and hot, together with a fine-particled nature, so that it very quickly melts away even flabby flesh in ulcers. It is also put into a drying drug. Burn it as you burn most other things: fill a new pot, then close it with a lid that has several holes.

11.1.30 [λ΄. Περὶ τριχῶν κεκαυμένων.] Τρίχες κεκαυμέναι παραπλησίως τοῖς κεκαυμένοις ἐρίοις θερμαντικῆς τε καὶ ξηραντικῆς ἱκανῶς εἰσι δυνάμεως. 11.1.31 [λα΄. Περὶ τῆς τῶν ὠῶν δυνάμεως.] Ἀκόλουθον ἂν εἴη τῇ περὶ τῶν μορίων διδασκαλίᾳ καὶ περὶ τῶν ὠῶν εἰπεῖν, ἀλλαχόθι γὰρ αὐτῶν οὐκ ἔχω μνημονεῦσαι. μέρη μὲν οὖν τῶν ζώων οὐκ ἔστι τὰ ὠὰ, καθάπερ οὐδὲ τῶν δένδρων ὁ καρπὸς, οὐδὲ τῶν κυουσῶν τὰ ἔμβρυα, τοῖς δ’ ἐπιφυομένοις ἀνάλογον ἔχει. φαίνεται δὲ τῶν ἐπιφυομένων τὰ μὲν οἰκεῖα πάντῃ ταῖς φύσεσιν τῶν φυτῶν ἢ ζώων εἶναι, τὰ δ’ οὐ πάντῃ τοιαῦτα, καθάπερ ὁ ἰξὸς ὁ ἐπιφυόμενος ταῖς δρυσὶν, οὐδ’ ἁπλῶς εἴ τι τοῖς δένδροις ἑτερογενὲς ἐπιφύεται δένδρον ἢ θάμνος ἢ καρπὸς ἢ πόα ἢ ὅλως ἐκβλάστημά τι, ﻿ καθάπερ οἱ μύκητες. ἐκ δ’ οὖν τῶν οἰκείων ἐστὶ καὶ τὸ ἔριον ἐπὶ τῶν προβάτων καὶ αἱ τρίχες ἐπὶ τῶν ἄλλων ζώων, καὶ τὰ κέρατα δὲ τὰ ἀποπίπτοντα καὶ πάλιν φυόμενα, καθάπερ ἐπὶ τῶν ἀῤῥένων ἐλάφων, ἐκ τούτου τοῦ γένους ἐστί. τὰ δὲ κυούμενα τοῖς καρποῖς ἀνάλογον ἔχει, διαδοχῆς ἕνεκα τοῦ γένους ὑπὸ τῆς φύσεως γινόμενα. τούτων ἕν τι καὶ τὸ ὠόν ἐστιν, ἕνεκα τῆς τοῦ γένους διαμονῆς γεγονός. ἔστι δὲ καὶ ὡς τροφὴ χρήσιμον, ἀλλὰ νῦν οὐ πρόκειται περὶ τροφῆς λέγειν. ὅσα τοίνυν ὡς φάρμακα ἐνεργεῖ, ταῦτ’ εἰρήσεται κατὰ τόνδε τὸν λόγον, ἐξ ὀλίγων τῶν κατὰ μέρος χρήσεων εὑρισκόντων ἡμῶν τὴν δύναμιν αὐτοῦ, κατά τε τὰ μόρια καὶ τὴν ὅλην οὐσίαν, ἐμάθομεν γὰρ ὅπως εὑρεθείσης τῆς καθόλου δυνάμεως ἑκάστου τῶν ὄντων ἐπὶ πολλὰς κατὰ μέρος χρήσεις ἄγειν αὐτὴν δυνάμεθα. προμεμαθήκαμεν δὲ καὶ ὅπως ἐκ μιᾶς ἢ δευτέρας πείρας ἐστὶ περὶ τῆς καθόλου δυνάμεως συλλογίσασθαι. τὸ μὲν οὖν λευκὸν καὶ λεπτὸν τῶν ὠῶν, ᾧ καὶ πρὸς τὰς ὀφθαλμίας χρώμεθα, τῶν ἀδηκτοτάτων ἐστὶ φαρμάκων, οὐδ’ ἀποτυχίαν εἶχον ἐκ δυσκρασίας τοιαύτης, οἵαν ἔχει τὸ γάλα πολλάκις, ὡς ἔμπροσθεν ἐδείκνυμεν ἐπὶ τῶν ἠπεπτηκότων ζώων ἢ ὑπερπεπεινηκότων ἢ ὑπερδεδιψηκότων ἢ μοχθηροῖς ἐδέσμασι κεχρημένων, ἃ δὴ καὶ μᾶλλον ἐπὶ γυναικῶν εἴωθε γίνεσθαι διὰ τὴν τῆς διαίτης πλημμέλειαν. τὰ δ’ ὠὰ μίαν μόνην ἐπιδέχεται μοχθηρίαν τὴν ἐκ τῆς παλαιότητος, ἣν ῥᾷστον φυλάττεσθαι τοῖς προσφάτοις χρώμενον. ἔστι δὲ συνηθέστατον ἡμῖν εὐποριστώτατόν τε τὸ τῶν ἀλεκτορίδων, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο αὐτῷ χρώμεθα μὴ δεόμενοι τῶν ἄλλων, καίτοι τὴν αὐτὴν ἐχόντων φύσιν. τῇ κράσει δ’ ἐστὶ ψυχρότερον τοῦ συμμέτρου. χρηστέον οὖν τῷ λευκῷ τῶν ὠῶν οὐ μόνον ἐπὶ τῶν ὀφθαλμῶν, ἀλλὰ καὶ ἐπὶ τῶν ἄλλων ἁπάντων ὅσα τῶν ἀδηκτοτάτων δεῖται φαρμάκων, ὡς τὰ καθ’ ἕδραν τε καὶ αἰδοῖον ἕλκη κακοήθη πάντα. μίγνυται δὲ χρησίμως καὶ τοῖς τὰς ἐκ μηνίγγων αἱμοῤῥαγίας ἐπέχουσι φαρμάκοις, ἅπερ ἐστὶν ἀδήκτως ἐμπλαττόμενά τε καὶ στύφοντα. καὶ πρὸς τὰ κακοήθη δὲ τῶν ἑλκῶν μίγνυται τοῖς ξηραίνειν ἀδήκτως ταῦτα πεφυκόσιν, ὁποῖόν ἐστι φάρμακον ὅ τε πεπλυμένος πομφόλυξ καὶ τῶν μεταλλικῶν ἔνια πλυθέντα, περὶ ὧν προείρηται κατὰ τὸν ἴδιον λόγον. ἔστι δὲ καὶ ἡ λέκιθος τῶν ὠῶν ὁμοίας φύσεως, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο μίγνυται κηρωταῖς ἀδήκτοις ἑψηθέντων ἢ ὀπτηθέντων τῶν ὠῶν. εὔδηλον δ’ ὅτι διοίσουσιν ἀλλήλων αὗται βραχεῖαν διαφορὰν διὰ τὸ ξηραντικώτερα μὲν ἠρέμα γίγνεσθαι τὴν ὄπτησιν, ὅσον δὲ προσέλαβε τῆς δυνάμεως ταύτης τοσοῦτον ἀπολλύναι τοῦ παρηγορικοῦ. μίγνυται δὲ καὶ καταπλάσμασιν ἀφλεγμάντοις, ὥσπερ ἐν ἕδρᾳ τοῖς διὰ μελιλώτου. ὠμῷ δ’ ὅλῳ ὠῷ χρώμεθα, μιγνύντες ῥόδινον ἐπὶ τῶν κατὰ βλέφαρα καὶ ὦτα καὶ τιτθοὺς φλεγμονῶν, ὅσαι τε πληγέντων αὐτῶν ἢ καὶ ἄλλως γίγνονται καὶ κατὰ τῶν νευρωδῶν δὲ σωμάτων, οἷον ἀγκῶνός τε καὶ τῶν κατὰ τοὺς δακτύλους τενόντων ἢ ἄρθρων ἐν ποσί τε καὶ χερσίν. ἐν ὄξει δ’ ἑψηθὲν ὠὸν βρωθείη, ξηραίνει τὰ κα τὰ γαστέρα ῥεύματα. καὶ εἰ μίξειας δ’ αὐτῷ τι τῶν πρὸς δυσεντερίαν ἢ κοιλιακὴν διάθεσιν ἁρμοττόντων, εἴτ’ ἐπ’ ἀκάπνου τε καὶ μετρίου πυρὸς, ὁποῖόν ἐστι τὸ ἐκ τῶν ἀνθράκων, ταγηνίσας δοίης φαγεῖν, οὐ σμικρότατα τοὺς κάμνοντας ὠφελήσεις. ἐπιτηδειότατα δὲ πρὸς τὴν τοιαύτην μίξιν ἐστὶν ὀμφάκιόν τε καὶ ῥοῦς ὅ τε τοῖς ὄψοις ἐπιπαττόμενος, ἐρυθρὸς ὀνομαζόμενος, ὅ τε χυλὸς αὐτοῦ κηκίς τε καὶ σίδια καὶ τέφρα τῶν ὀπτηθέντων ὅλων κοχλιῶν. ἐπιτήδεια δὲ καὶ γίγαρτα σταφυλῶν καὶ μύρτα καὶ μέσπιλα καὶ κράνα. φαρμακωδέστερα δὲ τούτων βαλαύστιόν τε καὶ ὑποκυστὶς καὶ κύτινοι. καὶ πρὸς κατακαύματα δὲ παραχρῆμα ὠμὸν ἐπιτιθέμενον ὠφέλιμόν ἐστιν, εἴτε τις ἀναλαβὼν ἐρίῳ μαλακῷ τὸ λευκὸν αὐτοῦ μόνον, εἴτε καὶ σὺν λεκίθῳ πᾶν ἀναδεύσας ἐπιθείη. καὶ γὰρ ἐμψύχει μετρίως καὶ ἀδήκτως ξηραίνει. τοιούτῳ δ’ ὄντι χρώμεθα αὐτῷ καὶ πρὸς τὰ κατὰ τοῦ μετώπου τῶν καλουμένων ἀνακολλημάτων ἐπιτιθέμενα καὶ τὰς ἐκ τῶν βλεφάρων δὲ τρίχας ἀνακολλῶμεν, ἐπιμιγνύντες τι τῶν ἐπιτηδείων, οἷός πέρ ἐστι καὶ ὁ λιβανωτὸς, καὶ μάλισθ’ ὅταν ᾖ λιπαρὸς καὶ μὴ παλαιὸς καὶ ξηρός. ἀλλ’ ἔν γε τοῖς τοιούτοις ἡ γλισχρότης τοῦ λευκοῦ τῶν ὠῶν ἐστι χρήσιμος, οὐχ ἡ κρᾶσις, εἰ μὴ καθ’ ὅσον οὐδὲν ἐναντιοῦται τῷ προσφερομένῳ φαρμάκῳ, χρήσιμον εἶναί τις αὐτὴν λέγοι. τινὰ γὰρ τῶν γλίσχρων ἐναντιοῦται, καθάπερ ὁ ἰξὸς δριμὺς καὶ θερμὸς ὢν εἰς τὰ μὴ δεόμενα θερμαίνεσθαι. καὶ λέλεκταί γε πολλάκις ὡς τὰ μηδεμίαν ἰσχυρὰν ἔχοντα δύναμιν φάρμακα μίγνυται τοῖς τὰς τοιαύτας ἔχουσιν δυνάμεις ὡς ὕλη καὶ διὰ τοῦτ’ ἔστι πολύχρηστα τοῖς ἰσχυρῶς ἐνεργοῦσιν ὑπηρετοῦντα. τοιοῦτον ὑπάρχον καὶ τὸ ὠὸν, ἅμα δὲ καὶ κατὰ τὴν τῆς ἑψήσεως ἢ ὀπτήσεως ποσότητα προσεπικτώμενον οὐ σμικρὰν διαφορὰν, πολύχρηστον γίνεται. τοῖς μὲν γὰρ ξηραίνουσι τὰς ὑγρότητας φαρμάκοις ἑψηθέν τε καὶ ὀπτηθὲν καὶ ταγηνισθὲν μίγνυται, τοῖς δὲ τὰ κατὰ θώρακα καὶ πνεύμονα τέμνουσιν τὸ καλούμενον ῥοφητὸν, ὃ μέχρι τοσούτου προσήκει δι’ ὕδατος ἕψειν, ὡς γενέσθαι θερμόν. κατὰ δὲ τὴν αὐτὴν φύσιν ἐπὶ τῶν τετραχυσμένων τὴν φάρυγγα διὰ κραυγὴν ἢ χυμοῦ δριμύτητα παραλαμβάνεται, περιπλαττόμενον μὲν τοῖς πεπονθόσι μέρεσι καὶ προσμένον ὥσπερ τι κατάπλασμα, τῷ δὲ τῆς οὐσίας ἀδήκτῳ παρηγοροῦν τε ἅμα καὶ ἐκθεραπεῦον αὐτά. τῷ δ’ αὐτῷ λόγῳ καὶ τὰς ﻿ κατὰ τὸν στόμαχον καὶ γαστέρα καὶ ἔντερα καὶ κύστιν ἰᾶται τραχύτητας.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: εἶχον, ῥοφητὸν, ὡς γενέσθαι θερμόν, ἀνακολλημάτων, κύτινοι
11.1.30 [30. On burnt hair.] Burnt hair, much like burnt wool, is of a sufficiently heating and drying power. 11.1.31 [31. On the power of eggs.] It would be in keeping with the teaching about the parts to speak also about eggs, for I have nowhere else to mention them. Eggs, then, are not parts of animals, just as the fruit is not a part of trees, nor the embryos of pregnant females; rather they stand in analogy to the things that grow upon them. Of the things that grow upon, some appear to be wholly proper to the natures of the plants or animals, others not wholly so, like the mistletoe that grows upon oaks, nor, simply, anything of a different kind that grows upon trees, whether tree or shrub or fruit or herb or in general some shoot, like mushrooms. Among the proper ones, then, is the wool on sheep and the hair on the other animals; and the horns too, those that fall off and grow again, as on male deer, belong to this class. But the things conceived stand in analogy to fruits, being produced by nature for the sake of the succession of the kind. One of these is also the egg, which has come into being for the sake of the continuance of the kind. It is also useful as food, but it is not now proposed to speak about food. Whatever, therefore, it effects as a drug, these things will be stated in this account, as we discover its power, both in respect of its parts and of its whole substance, from a few of the particular uses; for we have learned how, once the general power of each of the things that exist has been discovered, we are able to carry it over to many particular uses. And we have also learned beforehand how from one or a second trial it is possible to reason out the general power. Now the white and thin part of eggs, which we also use for ophthalmias, is among the least biting of drugs, nor have I met with failure from such a dyskrasia as milk often has, as we showed earlier in the case of animals that have failed to digest or have been excessively hungry or excessively thirsty or have used bad foods, things which indeed tend to occur more in women on account of faultiness of regimen. Eggs admit only one single badness, that from age, which is very easy to guard against by using fresh ones. The most customary for us and most easily procured is that of hens, and for this reason we use it, having no need of the others, although they have the same nature. In its mixture it is colder than the well-tempered. One must therefore use the white of eggs not only for the eyes but also for all the other conditions that need the least biting drugs, such as all the malignant ulcers at the seat and the genitals. It is also usefully mixed with the drugs that check hemorrhages from the meninges, which are drugs that plaster over without biting and are astringent. And for the malignant kinds of ulcers it is mixed with those that are naturally fitted to dry them without biting, such a drug as washed pompholyx and some of the metallic substances when washed, about which an account has been given earlier in their own section. The yolk of eggs too is of similar nature, and for this reason it is mixed into non-biting cerates, the eggs having been boiled or roasted. It is clear, however, that these will differ from one another by a slight difference, because roasting makes them gently more drying, and in so far as it has acquired this power, to that extent it loses of the soothing quality. It is also mixed into non-inflammatory poultices, as, for the seat, with those made with melilot. We use the whole raw egg, mixing in rose-oil, for inflammations about the eyelids and ears and breasts, both those that occur when these have been struck and otherwise, and for the sinewy bodies, such as the elbow and the tendons at the fingers, or the joints in feet and hands. An egg boiled in vinegar, if eaten, dries the fluxes of the belly. And if you were to mix with it one of the things suitable for dysentery or a coeliac condition, and, having fried it on a smokeless and moderate fire, such as that from charcoal, were to give it to eat, you will benefit the sufferers not in the least degree. Most suitable for such a mixture are omphakion and sumach, both the kind sprinkled on dishes, called the red, and its juice, as well as oak-gall and pomegranate rind and the ash of whole roasted snails. Suitable too are grape-stones and myrtle-berries and medlars and cornel-berries. More drug-like than these are balaustion and hypocistis and kytinoi. And for burns too it is beneficial applied raw at once, whether one takes up its white alone in soft wool, or soaks the whole together with the yolk and applies it. For it cools moderately and dries without biting. Being such, we use it also for the so-called anakollemata applied to the forehead, and we glue back the hairs of the eyelids, mixing in one of the suitable substances, such as frankincense is, especially when it is oily and not old and dry. But in cases of this kind it is the viscosity of the white of eggs that is useful, not its mixture, unless one were to say it is useful in so far as it in no way opposes the drug being applied. For some of the viscous substances do oppose, like mistletoe, which, being pungent and hot, is opposed to things that do not need to be heated. And it has often been said that drugs having no strong power are mixed as matter with those that have such powers, and for this reason are of manifold use, serving those that act strongly. The egg, being such, and at the same time acquiring in addition no small difference according to the degree of boiling or roasting, becomes of manifold use. For boiled and roasted and fried it is mixed with the drugs that dry moistures, while with those that cut what is in the chest and lung is mixed the so-called rhophēton, which ought to be boiled in water only so far as to become warm. In accordance with the same nature it is employed for those whose throat has been roughened through shouting or through the pungency of a humor, plastering itself around the affected parts and remaining there like a poultice, and by the non-biting quality of its substance both soothing them and curing them thoroughly. By the same reasoning it also heals the roughnesses in the stomach and belly and intestines and bladder.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: εἶχον, ὑπερπεπεινηκότων, ξηραντικώτερα μὲν ἠρέμα γίγνεσθαι τὴν ὄπτησιν, ἐμπλαττόμενα, ἀνακολλημάτων, κύτινοι, ἰξὸς (mistletoe vs. birdlime), ῥοφητὸν
11.1.30 [30. On burnt hairs.] Burnt hairs, like burnt wool, are of a fairly strong heating and drying power. 11.1.31 [31. On the power of eggs.] It would be fitting, as a sequel to the teaching about the parts, to speak also about eggs, for I have nowhere else to mention them. Eggs, then, are not parts of animals, just as fruit is not a part of trees, nor embryos of pregnant females; but they are analogous to outgrowths. Of outgrowths, some appear to be wholly proper to the natures of the plants or animals, others not wholly such: for example the mistletoe that grows on oaks, or, in general, anything of a different kind that grows on trees, whether tree or shrub or fruit or herb or in general some shoot, as mushrooms do. Among the proper ones, then, are the wool on sheep and the hairs on the other animals; and the horns that fall off and grow again, as in male deer, are also of this kind. Things conceived are analogous to fruits, being produced by nature for the sake of the succession of the kind. One of these is the egg, which has come into being for the sake of the preservation of the kind. It is also useful as food, but it is not now proposed to speak about food. Whatever, then, it effects as a drug will be stated in this discussion, as we discover its power from a few particular uses, both in respect of its parts and of its whole substance; for we have learned how, once the universal power of each existing thing has been found, we are able to extend it to many particular uses. And we have also learned beforehand how it is possible, from one or a second trial, to reason about the universal power. The white and thin part of eggs, which we also use for ophthalmias, is among the least biting of drugs, nor did they have any failure arising from such a dyscrasia as milk often has, as we showed earlier in the case of animals that have suffered indigestion, or have been excessively hungry or excessively thirsty, or have used bad foods, things which indeed tend to happen more in women because of error in regimen. Eggs admit only one defect, that from age, which is very easily guarded against by one who uses fresh ones. The most customary for us and the most easily obtained is that of hens, and for this reason we use it, having no need of the others, although they have the same nature. In its mixture it is colder than the well-balanced. One should therefore use the white of eggs not only for the eyes but also in all other cases that require the least biting drugs, such as all malignant ulcers at the seat and the genitals. It is also usefully mixed with the drugs that check haemorrhages from the meninges, which are those that plaster on without biting and are astringent. And for malignant ulcers it is mixed with those that by nature dry them without biting, of which kind of drug are washed pompholyx and some of the metallic substances when washed, about which it has been said before in the discussion proper to them. The yolk of eggs too is of a similar nature, and for this reason it is mixed into non-biting cerates, the eggs having been boiled or roasted. It is evident that these will differ from one another by a slight difference, because roasting makes them slightly more drying, and as much of this power as they have acquired, so much of the soothing they lose. It is also mixed into anti-inflammatory poultices, as at the seat into those made with melilot. We use the raw whole egg, mixing in rose oil, for inflammations at the eyelids, ears and breasts, whether they arise from these parts having been struck or otherwise, and also on sinewy bodies, such as the elbow and the tendons at the fingers, or the joints in the feet and hands. If an egg boiled in vinegar is eaten, it dries fluxes in the belly. And if you mix with it one of the things suited to dysentery or to the coeliac condition, and then fry it over a smokeless and moderate fire, such as that from coals, and give it to eat, you will benefit the patients not least. Most suitable for such a mixture are omphacium and sumach, the kind sprinkled on relishes, called red, and its juice, and oak-gall, and pomegranate rind, and the ash of whole roasted snails. Suitable also are grape-pips, myrtle berries, medlars and cornels. More drug-like than these are balaustion, hypocistis and cytini. For burns too, applied raw at once, it is beneficial, whether one takes up the white alone in soft wool, or moistens the whole together with the yolk and applies it. For it cools moderately and dries without biting. Since it is such, we use it also for the so-called anacollemata applied to the forehead, and we glue back the hairs of the eyelids, mixing in some suitable substance, such as frankincense, especially when it is oily and not old and dry. But in such cases it is the viscosity of the white of eggs that is useful, not its mixture, unless one were to say that the latter is useful insofar as it in no way opposes the drug applied. For some viscous things are opposed, as birdlime, being pungent and hot, is for things that do not need to be heated. And it has been said many times that drugs having no strong power are mixed with those having such powers as a matter, and for this reason they are of many uses, serving those that act strongly. The egg, being of this kind, and at the same time acquiring in addition no small difference according to the amount of boiling or roasting, becomes of many uses. For with drugs that dry up moistures it is mixed boiled, roasted and fried; but with those that cut the matters in the chest and lung, the so-called sippable egg, which should be boiled in water only so long as to become hot. According to the same nature it is employed for those whose throat has been roughened through shouting or through the pungency of a humour, being plastered around the affected parts and remaining there like a poultice, and through the non-biting quality of its substance at once soothing and healing them. By the same reasoning it also cures roughnesses in the stomach, belly, intestines and bladder.

11.1.33 [λγ΄. Περὶ κοχλιῶν.] Κοχλίαι δὲ ὅλοι, μετὰ τῶν ὀστράκων καιόμενοι μιγνυμένης αὐτοῖς κηκίδος ὀμφακίτιδος καὶ πεπέρεως λευκοῦ, μεγάλα ὠφελοῦσι δυσεντερίας, ἐφ’ ὧν οὐδέπω σηπεδονώδη τὰ ἕλκη. προσήκει δ’ εἶναι τοῦ μὲν πεπέρεως ἓν μέρος, τῆς δὲ κηκίδος δύο, τέσσαρα δὲ τῆς τέφρας τῶν κοχλιῶν. ἀκριβῶς λεῖα ποιήσας ταῦτα, τοῖς τ’ ὄψοις ἐπίπαττε καὶ δίδου πίνειν δι’ ὕδατος ἢ οἴνου λευκοῦ τε καὶ αὐστηροῦ. χωρὶς δὲ τοῦ μιχθῆναι κηκίδι, κοχλιῶν ἡ τέφρα ξηραντικῆς ἱκανῶς ἐστι δυνάμεως, ἐχούσης τι διὰ τὴν καῦσιν καὶ θερμοῦ. ἄκαυστοι δ’ οἱ κοχλίαι λειωθέντες ἅμα τοῖς ὀστράκοις ἐπιτίθενται κατά τε τῆς γαστρὸς ὅλης ἐπὶ τῶν ὑδερικῶν, κατά τε τῶν ἐν τοῖς ἄρθροις ὄγκων ἐπὶ τῶν ἀρθριτικῶν. καὶ γίνεται μὲν ἡ πρόθεσις αὐτῶν δυσαφαίρετος, ἱκανῶς δὲ ξηραίνει. καὶ μέντοι καὶ προσκεῖσθαι διὰ παντὸς ἐᾷν αὐτοὺς προσῆκεν, ἄχρις ἂν ἀποπέσωσιν αὐτόματα. τοῦτο δὲ ποιητέον κᾀπὶ τῶν δυσλύτων ἐκ πληγῆς ὄγκων καὶ περὶ θλάσεως γενομένης ἐν ὠσί. ξηραίνουσι γὰρ ἱκανῶς ἅπαντας αὐτοὺς, κᾂν γλίσχρον καὶ παχὺ κατὰ βάθους ὑγρὸν περιέχηται.
Rendering A (fable-5-1)
11.1.33 [33. On snails.] Snails, whole, burnt together with their shells, with omphakitis gall-nut and white pepper mixed in with them, are of great benefit in dysenteries in which the ulcers are not yet putrid. The proportion should be one part of the pepper, two of the gall-nut, and four of the ash of the snails. Having made these thoroughly smooth, sprinkle them on the foods and give them to drink in water or in wine that is white and harsh. Without being mixed with gall-nut, the ash of snails is of a sufficiently drying power, which also has, on account of the burning, something of heat. Unburnt, the snails, pounded smooth together with their shells, are applied over the whole belly in the case of the dropsical, and over the swellings in the joints in the case of the arthritic. And their application becomes hard to remove, but it dries sufficiently. And indeed one ought to allow them to remain applied throughout, until they fall off of their own accord. This is also to be done in the case of swellings from a blow that are hard to resolve, and in the case of a contusion that has occurred in the ears. For they dry all of these sufficiently, even if a viscous and thick fluid is contained in the depth.

11.1.33 [33. On snails.] Whole snails, burnt together with their shells, with omphacitis gall and white pepper mixed in with them, are of great benefit in dysenteries, in those cases where the ulcers are not yet putrefying. There should be one part of the pepper, two of the gall, and four of the ash of the snails. Having made these thoroughly smooth, sprinkle them on the cooked dishes and give them to drink in water or in wine that is white and harsh. Apart from being mixed with gall, the ash of snails is of a sufficiently drying power, which also has something of heat on account of the burning. Unburnt snails, ground smooth together with their shells, are applied both over the whole belly in the dropsical and over the swellings in the joints in the arthritic. Their application becomes hard to remove, but it dries sufficiently. And indeed it is proper to let them remain applied the whole time, until they fall off of their own accord. This is to be done also in the case of swellings from a blow that are hard to resolve, and with a contusion that has occurred in the ears. For they sufficiently dry all of these, even if a viscous and thick fluid is contained in the depth.

  • B · drug names: named in the Greek only (the name of the drug of entry 9.3.24)
    ἄκαυστοι δ’ οἱ κοχλίαι λειωθέντες ἅμα τοῖς ὀστράκοις ἐπιτίθενται κατά τε τῆς γαστρὸς ὅλης ἐπὶ τῶν ὑδερικῶν, κατά τε τῶν ἐν τοῖς ἄρθροις ὄγκων ἐπὶ τῶν ἀρθριτικῶν.
    Unburnt snails, ground smooth together with their shells, are applied both over the whole belly in the dropsical and over the swellings in the joints in the arthritic.

11.1.34 [λδ΄. Περὶ καρκίνων κεκαυμένων.] Ἥ γε μὴν τῶν ποταμίων καρκίνων τέφρα ξηραντικὴ μέν ἐστιν ὁμοίως τοῖς προειρημένοις, ἰδιότητι δὲ τῆς ὅλης οὐσίας θαυμαστῶς ἐπὶ τῶν λυσσοδήκτων ἐνεργεῖ, καὶ μόνη μὲν, ἀλλὰ καὶ μετὰ γεντιανῆς τε καὶ λιβανωτοῦ πολὺ μᾶλλον. εἶναι δὲ χρὴ τοῦ μὲν λιβανωτοῦ μοῖραν μίαν, πέντε δὲ τῆς γεντιανῆς καὶ τῶν καρκίνων δέκα. καὶ ἄλλως μὲν οὖν καυθεῖσιν αὐτοῖς ἐχρησάμεθά ποτε σπανίως, ὡς τὸ πολὺ δὲ καθ’ ὃν Αἰσχρίων ὁ ἐμπειρικὸς ἐχρήσατο φαρμάκων ἐμπειρικώτατος γέρων, πολίτης τε καὶ διδάσκαλος ἡμέ τερος. ἦν δὲ λοπὰς ἐρυθροῦ χαλκοῦ, καθ’ ἧς ἐπιτιθεὶς ζῶντας τοὺς καρκίνους ἔκαε ἄχρις οὗ τεφρωθῶσιν, ὡς εὐκόλως λειοῦσθαι. οὗτος ὁ Αἰσχρίων εἶχεν ἀεὶ παρεσκευασμένον ἕτοιμον ἐπὶ τῆς οἰκίας τὸ φάρμακον, ὥρᾳ θέρους κάων τοὺς καρκίνους, μετὰ τὴν τοῦ κυνὸς ἐπιτολὴν, ἡνίκα λέοντι ἥλιος ἦν, ἡ σελήνη δὲ ὀκτωκαιδεκαταία. πίνειν τε καθ’ ἑκάστην ἡμέραν ἐδίδου τὸ φάρμακον τοῦτο τοῖς λυσσοδήκτοις, ἄχρι τῆς τεσσαρακοστῆς ἐπιπάσσων ὕδατι κοχλιάριον εὐμέγεθες. εἰ δ’ οὐκ ἐξ ἀρχῆς, ἀλλὰ μεθ’ ἡμέρας τινὰς τοῦ δηχθῆναι προὐνοεῖτο τοῦ δεδηγμένου, δύο κοχλιάρια καθ’ ἑκάστην ἡμέραν ἐπέπαττεν. ἐχρῆτο δὲ καὶ κατ’ αὐτοῦ τραύματος τῷ διὰ τῆς Βρυτίας πίττης, ὀποπάνακός τε καὶ ὄξους, ἐμπλαστικῷ φαρμάκῳ, μίαν λαμβάνοντι πίττης λίτραν, ἕνα δὲ ὄξους δριμυτάτου ξέστην Ἰταλικὸν, ὀποπάνακος δὲ τρεῖς οὐγγίας. ταῦτα καίτοι τῆς προκειμένης οὐκ ὄντα πραγματείας, ἔγραψα διὰ τὸ θαῤῥεῖν τῷ φαρμάκῳ, μηδενὸς μηδέποτε ἀποθανόντος τῶν ὡς εἴρηται χρησαμένων αὐτῷ. ποιήσομαι δὲ καὶ κατὰ μόνας ἑτέραν. πραγματείαν περὶ τῶν ἰδιότητι τῆς ὅλης οὐσίας ἐνεργούντων, ἐν οἷς ἐστι καὶ τὰ τοιαῦτα πάντα. συγγινώσκειν οὖν χρὴ τῷ τῆς γραφῆς ἀκαίρῳ καὶ νῦν καὶ κατ’ ἄλλα χωρία τῆσδε τῆς πραγματείας ἐνίοτε γεγονότι, διὰ τὴν ἐκ τῶν λεγομένων ὠφέλειαν μεγίστην οὖσαν, ἣν διασώζεσθαι βούλομαι τοῖς μεθ’ ἡμᾶς ἀνθρώποις, εἰ καὶ μεταξὺ θάνατος γενόμενος ἀποκωλύσει με γράψαι τὰς ἐφεξῆς τῆσδε τῆς πραγματείας. ἁπάντων δὲ τῶν τοιούτων τὰς αἰτίας λέγειν βουλόμενος ὁ διδάσκαλος ἡμῶν Πέλοψ εἰκότως ἔφη τὸν καρκίνον, ἔνυδρον ζῶον ὑπάρχοντα, ὠφελεῖν τοὺς λυσσοδήκτους, οἷς φόβος ἐστὶν ἁλῶναι πάθει ξηροτάτῳ τῇ λύττῃ, διὸ καὶ τὸ ὕδωρ φοβοῦνται. ποταμίους δὲ τοὺς καρκίνους, οὐ θαλαττίους, ἔφασκεν ἁρμόττειν, ἐπειδὴ διὰ τὴν ἐπιμιξίαν τῶν ἁλῶν, ξηραντικωτάτων ὄντων, τὰ θαλάττια ζῶα τὴν πρὸς τὴν λύτταν ἐναντιότητα μὴ διαφυλάξειεν ἀκριβῆ. καί τινος εἰπόντος αὐτῷ, διὰ τί οὖν οὐχὶ καὶ πάντα τὰ ἐν ὕδατι ποτίμῳ ζῶα παραπλησίως τοῖς καρκίνοις ὠφελεῖ; ὅτι, ἔφη, τὴν ὁμοίαν σκευασίαν τοῖς καρκίνοις οὐ δύναται δέξασθαι. τούτων γὰρ καυθέντων τὴν τέφραν, ξηραντικὴν γινομένην, ἐκδαπανᾷν τε ἅμα καὶ διαφορεῖν τὸν ἰὸν τῶν δακνόντων κυνῶν. ταῦτα μὲν οὖν ὁ Πέλοψ ἔλεγεν, ἐπαγγελλόμενός τε καὶ φιλοτιμούμενος ἐπίστασθαι τὰς αἰτίας αὐτῶν ἁπάντων. ἐγὼ δὲ ἐὰν μὴ πρότερον ἐμαυτὸν πείσω γινώσκειν ἀκριβῶς τι, τοὺς πέλας οὐκ ἐπιχειρῶ πείθειν. οὐκοῦν οὐδὲ τὸν τοῦ Πέλοπος λόγον ὡς ἀληθῆ προσηκάμην, ἀντιλογίας ἔχοντα συχνὰς, ἀλλὰ καὶ τοὺς καρκίνους ἡγοῦμαι κατὰ τὴν ἰδιότητα τῆς ὅλης οὐσίας ὠφελεῖν. διὰ δὲ τὸ μηδένα τεθνάναι τῶν χρησαμένων αὐτοῖς, τοῖς γε μὴν σώμασιν ὅλοις ἐβουλήθην ἤδη δεδηλῶσθαί μοι τοῦτο, κᾂν μὴ τῆς ἐνεστώσης πραγματείας οἰκεῖον ᾖ. τοῦτο γὰρ οὐκ ἦν τὸ προκείμενον.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τοῖς γε μὴν σώμασιν ὅλοις, ἐβουλήθην ἤδη δεδηλῶσθαί μοι τοῦτο
11.1.34 [34. On burnt crabs.] The ash of river crabs, for its part, is drying in the same way as the things mentioned before, but by the peculiarity of its whole substance it acts wonderfully in the case of those bitten by rabid dogs, both on its own, but also much more together with gentian and frankincense. There should be one part of frankincense, five of gentian, and ten of the crabs. Now we have on occasion, though rarely, used them burnt in another manner, but for the most part in the manner in which Aeschrion the Empiricist used them, an old man most experienced in drugs, a fellow citizen and teacher of ours. There was a dish of red copper, upon which he set the crabs alive and burnt them until they were reduced to ash, so as to be ground fine easily. This Aeschrion always had the drug prepared and ready in his house, burning the crabs in the season of summer, after the rising of the Dog-star, when the sun was in Leo and the moon was eighteen days old. And he gave this drug to those bitten by rabid dogs to drink every day up to the fortieth, sprinkling a good-sized spoonful into water. But if he was taking care of the bitten person not from the beginning but some days after the bite, he sprinkled in two spoonfuls each day. He also used upon the wound itself the clogging drug made from Bruttian pitch, opopanax and vinegar, which takes one litra of pitch, one Italian xestes of the most pungent vinegar, and three ounces of opopanax. These things, although they do not belong to the present treatise, I have written because of my confidence in the drug, since no one who has used it as described has ever died. I shall also compose separately another treatise on the things that act by the peculiarity of their whole substance, among which are all such things as these. One must therefore pardon the untimeliness of the writing, both now and where it has sometimes occurred in other places of this treatise, because of the benefit from what is said, which is very great and which I wish to be preserved for the people who come after us, even if death intervening should prevent me from writing the treatises that follow this one. Our teacher Pelops, wishing to state the causes of all such things, said plausibly that the crab, being an aquatic animal, benefits those bitten by rabid dogs, who are in fear of being seized by a most dry affection, rabies, on account of which they also fear water. And he said that river crabs, not sea crabs, are suitable, since, because of the admixture of salts, which are most drying, sea animals would not preserve exactly their opposition to rabies. And when someone said to him, why then do not all the animals living in drinkable water help in like manner to the crabs?, he said: because they cannot receive the same preparation as the crabs. For when these are burnt their ash, becoming drying, both consumes and at the same time disperses the venom of the biting dogs. These things, then, Pelops used to say, professing and priding himself on knowing the causes of all of them. But I, unless I have first persuaded myself that I know something accurately, do not attempt to persuade my neighbours. Accordingly I have not accepted Pelops' account as true either, since it admits of numerous counter-arguments, but I hold that the crabs too help by the peculiarity of their whole substance. But because none of those who used them has died, with their whole bodies at any rate [τοῖς γε μὴν σώμασιν ὅλοις], I wished this to have been made clear by me already, even if it is not proper to the present treatise. For this was not what was proposed.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: καθ’ ὃν (sc. τρόπον?), ἡμέ τερος (split ἡμέτερος), ἑτέραν. πραγματείαν (stray stop), προὐνοεῖτο, ἐμπλαστικῷ φαρμάκῳ (rendered 'plaster drug', not in the technical sense 'clogging'), λύττῃ / λύτταν (rendered 'lytta'), ἰόν, τοῖς γε μὴν σώμασιν ὅλοις
11.1.34 [34. On burnt crabs.] The ash of river crabs is drying, like the things mentioned before, but by a property of its whole substance it acts wonderfully in those bitten by a rabid animal, both on its own and, much more, together with gentian and frankincense. There should be one part of the frankincense, five of the gentian and ten of the crabs. On occasion, and rarely, we have used them burnt in other ways, but for the most part in the way that Aeschrion the Empiricist used them, an old man most experienced in drugs, our fellow citizen and teacher. It was a dish of red copper, on which he placed the crabs alive and burned them until they were reduced to ash, so that they could easily be ground fine. This Aeschrion always had the drug prepared and ready in his house, burning the crabs in the summer season, after the rising of the Dog Star, when the sun was in Leo and the moon was in its eighteenth day. He gave this drug to drink every day to those bitten by a rabid animal, up to the fortieth day, sprinkling a good-sized spoonful into water. But if he took charge of the bitten person not from the beginning but some days after the bite, he sprinkled in two spoonfuls each day. On the wound itself he also used the plaster drug made with Bruttian pitch, opopanax and vinegar, which takes one litra of pitch, one Italian xestes of the most pungent vinegar, and three ounces of opopanax. Although these things do not belong to the present treatise, I have written them because of my confidence in the drug, since no one of those who used it in the way described has ever died. I shall also compose separately another treatise on the things that act by a property of the whole substance, among which are all things of this kind. One must therefore pardon the untimeliness of the writing, both now and in other places of this treatise where it sometimes occurs, on account of the benefit from what is said, which is very great, and which I wish to be preserved for the people after us, even if death intervening should prevent me from writing the next parts of this treatise. Our teacher Pelops, wishing to state the causes of all such things, said that the crab, being an aquatic animal, reasonably benefits those bitten by a rabid animal, who are in fear of being seized by lytta, a most dry affection, which is why they also fear water. He said that river crabs, not sea crabs, are suitable, since because of the admixture of salts, which are most drying, sea animals would not preserve an exact contrariety to lytta. And when someone said to him, 'Why then do not all the animals in drinkable water benefit similarly to the crabs?', 'Because,' he said, 'they cannot receive a preparation similar to that of the crabs. For when these are burnt, their ash, becoming drying, both consumes and at the same time disperses the venom of the biting dogs.' These, then, were the things Pelops said, professing and priding himself on knowing the causes of them all. But I, unless I first persuade myself that I know something exactly, do not attempt to persuade my neighbours. Accordingly I did not accept Pelops' argument as true either, since it is open to many objections, but I hold that the crabs too benefit by the property of the whole substance. But because none of those who used them has died, I wished this to have been made clear by me already, with the whole bodies at any rate, even if it is not proper to the present treatise. For this was not what was proposed.

11.1.35 [35. On burnt swallows.] Not in the case of those animals alone which I have mentioned, but of swallows too many have made use, burning them and mixing honey with the ash, then smearing it on those suffering from synanche, and in general on all the affections that arise in the pharynx and uvula together with swellings. Some people also use this ash for sharpness of sight. Others dry them out like mummies and give them to drink in the quantity of a drachm.

11.1.35 [35. On burnt swallows.] Not only in the case of the animals I have mentioned: many have also used swallows, burning them and mixing honey with the ash, then anointing those suffering from synanche with it; and in general all the affections that occur in the pharynx and the uvula accompanied by swellings. Some people also use this ash for sharpness of sight. Others, drying them out [σκελετεύοντες, literally 'desiccating', 'mummifying'], give them to drink in the quantity of a drachm.

11.1.37 [λζ΄. Περὶ κορύδων.] Ὅ γε μὴν κόρυδος τὸ πτηνὸν ζῶον τουτὶ τὸ μικρὸν, ὃ καὶ κατὰ τὰς ὁδοὺς πολλάκις ὁρῶμεν, ἑψόμενον ἐν τῷ ζωμῷ τοὺς κωλικοὺς ὠφελεῖ. χρὴ δὲ συνεχῶς καὶ πολλάκις ἐσθίειν αὐτὸ μετὰ τοῦ ζωμοῦ. ἔχει δὲ ἐπὶ τῆς κεφαλῆς τοῦτο τὸ ζῶον ὥσπερ τινὰ λόφον ἐκ τῶν τριχῶν αὐτοφυῆ, δι’ ὃν καὶ ὁ μῦθος ὃν Ἀριστοφάνης ὁ κωμικὸς ἔγραψεν ἐπλάσθη. λέγει δὲ περὶ αὐτοῦ τόνδε τὸν τρόπον· ἀμαθὴς γὰρ ἔφυς καὶ πολυπράγμων, οὐδ’ Αἴσωπον μεμάθηκας, ὃς ἔφασκε λέγων κόρυδον πρώτην πάντων ὀρνίθων γενέσθαι, πρότερον τῆς γῆς, κᾄπειτα νόσῳ τὸν πατέρα αὐτῆς ἀποθνήσκειν, γῆν δ’ οὐκ εἶναι, αὐτὸν δὲ προκεῖσθαι πεμπταῖον, τὴν δ’ ἀποροῦσαν ὑπ’ ἀμηχανίας τὸν πατέρα αὐτῆς ἐν τῇ κεφαλῇ κατορύξαι. τοῦτο δέ φασι καὶ τὸν Θεόκριτον αἰνίττεσθαι λέγοντα, Οὐδ’ ἐπιτυμβίδιαι κορυδαλλίδες ἠλαίνοντι. δηλοῦν γὰρ αὐτὸν τὰς τὸν τύμβον ἐπὶ τῆς κεφαλῆς ἐχούσας. προσέθηκα δὲ ταῦτα τῷ λόγῳ, διὰ τὸ βούλεσθαι σαφῶς δηλῶσαι τὸ ζῶον τὸν κόρυδον, ὁποίαν τινὰ τὴν ἐπὶ τῆς κεφαλῆς ἀνάστασιν ἔχει τῶν τριχῶν, ἐπειδὴ τῆς ὠφελείας αὐτοῦ πρὸς τοὺς κωλικοὺς ἐπειράθην, καὶ βούλομαι καλῶς γνωρίσαι τοῖς οὐκ εἰδόσιν αὐτὸ, ἐπειδὴ τοῖς πυργήτοις ὀνομαζομένοις ἔοικεν, ἀλλὰ τῷ τε λόφῳ διακρίνεται καὶ βραχύ τι μείζων ἐστὶν αὐτοῦ. καὶ γάρ τοι καὶ ἡ κορυδαλὶς καλουμένη βοτάνη καὶ αὐτοῖς κωλικοῖς ἁρμόττει. καί σοι καὶ τοῦτο κατὰ τὸ πάρεργον ἐγνώσθω.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἠλαίνοντι, πυργήτοις, ἐπιτυμβίδιαι
11.1.37 [37. On larks.] The lark, this small winged animal which we often see also along the roads, when boiled in broth benefits colic patients. One must eat it continually and often together with the broth. This animal has on its head a sort of crest, as it were, of naturally grown hairs, on account of which the fable which Aristophanes the comic poet wrote was also fashioned. He speaks about it in the following manner: "For you are by nature ignorant and meddlesome, and you have not learned your Aesop, who said in his tale that the lark came into being first of all birds, before the earth, and that then her father died of a disease, but there was no earth, and he lay exposed for five days; and she, at a loss for want of any means, buried her father in her own head." This they say Theocritus too hints at when he says, "Nor do the tomb-bearing crested larks roam about"; for he means those that have the tomb upon their head. I have added these things to my account because I wish to indicate clearly the animal the lark, what sort of upstanding crest of hairs it has upon its head, since I have made trial of its benefit for colic patients, and I wish to make it well known to those who do not know it, since it resembles the birds called pyrgitai, but is distinguished by its crest and is a little larger than that bird. And indeed the plant called korydalis too is suitable for these same colic patients. And let this too be known to you as a by-the-way.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: πυργήτοις, καὶ πολυπράγμων, πεμπταῖον, ἠλαίνοντι, αὐτοῖς
11.1.37 [37. On crested larks.] The crested lark, this small winged creature that we often see along the roads, benefits colic sufferers when it is boiled in broth. One must eat it continually and often, together with the broth. This creature has on its head something like a crest of naturally occurring hairs, and it was on account of this crest that the fable which Aristophanes the comic poet wrote was invented. He speaks of it in this way: 'For you are by nature ignorant and a busybody, and you have not learned your Aesop, who used to say that the crested lark came into being first of all birds, before the earth, and that then her father died of a disease, but there was no earth, and he lay out for five days, and she, at a loss and having no other means, buried her father in her head.' They say that Theocritus too alludes to this when he says, 'Nor are the tomb-crested larks roaming about.' For they say he means those that have the tomb on their head. I have added these things to the account because I wish to show clearly what kind of raising of the hairs on its head the creature, the crested lark, has. I have tested its benefit for colic sufferers, and I wish to make it properly known to those who do not know it, since it resembles the birds called pyrgētoi, but it is distinguished by its crest and is a little larger than it. Indeed the herb called korydalis is also suitable for colic sufferers themselves. Let this too be known to you, by the way.

11.1.39 [39. On the weasel.] A weasel, however, we have never burnt, though they have written of it that its ash, smeared on with vinegar, benefits gouty and arthritic patients, since it is by nature capable of dispersing sufficiently, and that, dried out and drunk, it benefits epileptics, evidently in virtue of the same power. Περὶ ταριχηρῶν τελλινῶν.] Καὶ τὰς ταριχηρὰς δὲ τελλίνας καυθείσας καυστικὴν ἐργάζεσθαι τέφραν εἰς τοσοῦτόν φασιν, ὥστε μετὰ κεδρίας μιγνυμένην αὐτὴν, ὅταν ἐκ τῶν ῥιζῶν ἐξέλωμεν τὰς τρίχας τῶν βλεφάρων, ἐνσταζομένην τῷ χωρίῳ κωλύειν αὖθις αὐτὰς ἀναφύεσθαι. Περὶ γῆς ἐντέρων. Χωρὶς δὲ καύσεως ἢ ἑψήσεως ὅλων τῶν ζώων ἐπιτιθεμένων τοῖς πεπονθόσι μορίοις ἢ εἴσω τοῦ σώματος λαμβανομένων ἐπειράθησαν, ἢ ὡς πεπειραμένοι γεγράφασιν ἔνιοι τῶν ἰατρῶν γῆς μὲν ἔντερα λειωθέντα, νεύροις διῃρημένοις ἐπιτιθέμενα, παραχρῆμα θαυμαστῶς ὀνινάναι, τὰ δ’ αὐτὰ ταῦτα πάντα σὺν γλυκεῖ πινόμενα φάρμακον οὐρητικὸν εἶναι.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: ἐπειράθησαν
11.1.42 [42. On salted tellins.] And salted tellins too, when burnt, they say, produce an ash so caustic that, when it is mixed with cedar-oil and dripped onto the spot after we have pulled out the hairs of the eyelids from the roots, it prevents them from growing back again. On earthworms. Without burning or boiling, people have made trial of whole animals, either applied to the affected parts or taken into the body—or some of the physicians have written as though they had made trial of them: earthworms, ground fine and applied to divided sinews, give immediate and marvellous help, and these same ones, all of them taken as a drink with sweet wine, are a diuretic drug.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: κεδρίας, τὰ δ’ αὐτὰ ταῦτα πάντα, νεύροις διῃρημένοις
11.1.42 [42. On salted tellins.] They say also that salted tellins, when burnt, produce an ash so caustic that, mixed with cedar-resin and dropped into the spot when we have pulled the hairs of the eyelids out from their roots, it prevents them from growing again. On earthworms. Some of the physicians have made trial, or have written as though they had made trial, of whole animals applied to the affected parts, or taken into the body, without burning or boiling: that earthworms (literally 'intestines of the earth'), ground smooth and applied to severed nerves, help at once in a wonderful way, and that these same things, all of them, drunk with sweet wine, are a diuretic drug.

11.1.44 [μδ΄. Περὶ κανθαρίδων.] Κανθαρίδων γε μὴν ἱκανὴν πεῖραν ἔχομεν ἐπὶ τῶν ψωρωδῶν ὀνύχων, οἷς ἐπιτιθέμεναι μετὰ τῶν ἐπιτηδείων κηρωτῶν ἢ ἐμπλαστῶν φαρμάκων ἐξάγουσιν αὐτοὺς, ὡς ἐκπίπτειν ὅλους. ἐμίξαμεν δὲ καὶ ταῖς πρὸς ψώρας καὶ λέπρας ἁρμοζούσαις δυνάμεσι καί τισι τῶν σηπτῶν, ἐνίοτε δὲ καὶ τῶν τοὺς καλουμένους ἥλους ἐκβαλλόντων. εἷς δέ τις τῶν ἡμετέρων διδασκάλων ἐνέβαλλεν αὐτῶν ὀλίγον εἰς οὐρητικὸν φάρμακον. ἔνιοι δὲ μόνα τὰ πτερὰ καὶ τοὺς πόδας ἐμβάλλουσιν, ἃ δὴ καὶ ἀλεξητήριά φασιν εἶναι τοῖς τὰ τώματα πιοῦσιν αὐτῶν. ἔνιοι δὲ ἔμπαλιν, ἡμεῖς δὲ ὅλας ἐμβάλλομεν. ἐπιτηδειότεραι δ’ εἰσὶ πρὸς ἅπερ ἔφην λέγων πεπειράσθαι τῶν κανθαρίδων ἐκ τῶν πυρῶν ὅσαι μηλίνας ἐγκαρσίας ζώνας ἔχουσιν ἐν τοῖς πτεροῖς, καὶ μᾶλλον, ἐὰν ἐμβαλὼν αὐτὰς εἰς ἀγγεῖον καινὸν κεράμιον, εἶτα περιτιθεὶς τῷ στόματι τοῦ ἀγγείου τῶν ἀραιῶν ὀθονίων τι κρατῇς οὕτω τὸ ἀγγεῖον, ὡς ἐστράφθαι τὴν ὀθόνην κάτω, δεχομένην τὸν ἀναφερόμενον ἀτμὸν τοῦ ὄξους ἄχρις ἂν ἀποθάνωσιν αἱ κανθαρίδες.
Rendering A (fable-5-1) · unsure: τώματα, ἐκ τῶν πυρῶν
11.1.44 [44. On cantharides.] Of cantharides, however, we have sufficient experience in the case of scabby (psora-like) nails, to which, when applied together with the suitable cerates or plaster-drugs, they draw the nails out, so that they fall off whole. We have also mixed them into the compositions suited to psorai and lepra, and into some of the putrefying drugs, and sometimes also into those that expel the so-called corns (heloi). One of our teachers used to put a little of them into a diuretic drug. Some people put in only the wings and the feet, which indeed they say are also protective remedies for those who have drunk their bodies (tōmata); others do the opposite, but we put them in whole. The more suitable among the cantharides, for the purposes for which I said I had made trial of them, are those from the wheat which have quince-yellow transverse bands on their wings, and more so if, having put them into a new earthenware vessel and then fastened one of the loose-woven linen cloths around the mouth of the vessel, you hold the vessel in such a way that the linen is turned downward, receiving the vapour of the vinegar rising up, until the cantharides die.
Rendering B (opus-5-5) · unsure: τώματα (apparently corrupt; read as σώματα, 'bodies'), δυνάμεσι (here 'compositions/preparations'), ἐκ τῶν πυρῶν (taken as 'from the wheat'), μηλίνας (yellow / quince-yellow), ἀραιῶν ὀθονίων (loose-woven linen)
11.1.44 [44. On cantharides.] Of cantharides, at any rate, we have ample experience in the case of psoric nails: applied to these together with suitable cerates or plasters, they draw the nails out, so that they fall off entire. We have also mixed them into the compositions (δυνάμεσι) suited to psorai and leprai, and into some of the putrefying drugs, and sometimes also into those that expel the so-called corns. One of our teachers used to put a little of them into a diuretic drug. Some people put in only the wings and the feet, which they also say are a remedy for those who have drunk their bodies (τὰ τώματα); others do the reverse; but we put them in whole. More suitable for the purposes for which I said I had tested cantharides are those from the wheat that have transverse yellow bands on their wings; and they are more so if you put them into a new earthenware vessel, then place some piece of loose-woven linen over the mouth of the vessel, and hold the vessel in such a way that the linen is turned downwards, receiving the vapour of the vinegar that rises up, until the cantharides die.

11.1.48 [μη΄. Περὶ νάρκης.] Καὶ τὴν νάρκην δ’ ὅλην, λέγω δὲ δηλονότι τὸ θαλάττιον ζῶον, ἔγραψαν ἔνιοι κεφαλαλγίας ἰᾶσθαι προσφερομένην τῇ κεφαλῇ καὶ τὴν ἐξεστραμμένην ἕδραν ἐντρέπειν. ἀλλ’ ἐγὼ πειραθεὶς ἀμφοῖν οὐδέτερον ἐργαζομένην εὗρον. ἐννοήσας οὖν αὐτὴν ἔτι ζῶσαν προστιθέναι τινὶ κεφαλὴν ὀδυνωμένῳ, δύνασθαι γὰρ ἀνώδυνον εἶναι φάρμακον, ὁμοίως τοῖς ἄλλοις ὅσα ναρκοῖ τὴν αἴσθησιν εὗρον οὕτως ἔχειν. οἴομαι δὲ καὶ τὸν πρῶτον χρησάμενον ἔκ τινος τοιαύτης ἐπινοίας ὁρμηθέντα χρῆσθαι.

11.1.48 [48. On the torpedo.] And some people have written that the torpedo too, whole—I mean, obviously, the sea animal—cures headaches when applied to the head, and turns back a prolapsed anus. But I, having made trial of both, found that it brings about neither. So, having conceived the idea of applying it, while still alive, to someone suffering pain in the head—for it could be a pain-stilling drug, like the other things that numb sensation—I found that this is so. And I think that the first person to use it also set out to use it prompted by some such notion.

11.1.48 [48. On the torpedo.] Some people have also written that the torpedo as a whole (I mean, of course, the sea animal) cures headaches when applied to the head, and turns back in a prolapsed (everted) anus. Then I conceived the idea of applying it, while still alive, to someone suffering pain in the head, reasoning that it could be an anodyne drug, like the other things that numb sensation, and I found that this was so. I think that the first person who used it was also led to use it by some such notion.
